## Independent generalized reflected-ray and GCC convention

[Open item](item-prop-se-generalized-ray-contract) · level 0


### Statement

Let $D\subset\mathbb R^d$, $d\ge2$, have smooth boundary. A cotangent vector is a linear functional on the tangent space; cotangent vectors over all points form the cotangent bundle. On spacetime $Y=\mathbb R_t\times\overline D$, use the wave symbol $p(t,x,\tau,\xi)=-\tau^2+|\xi|^2$ and its nonzero characteristic covectors, meaning $p=0$ and $(\tau,\xi)\ne0$. In local boundary normal coordinates $y=(y',y_n)$, where $y_n>0$ is the domain side and $y'$ includes time, write $p=\eta_n^2+r(y,\eta')$ and $r_0=r|_{y_n=0}$. The compression map is $j(y;\eta)=(y;\eta',y_n\eta_n)$, with the natural compressed-bundle topology. At the boundary it identifies covectors having the same tangential components, including the incoming and outgoing characteristic covectors. Boundary characteristic points are hyperbolic when $r_0<0$ and glancing when $r_0=0$.

A complete generalized bicharacteristic is a differentiable characteristic curve $\gamma:\mathbb R\setminus B\to T^*Y$, with $B\subset\mathbb R$ consisting of isolated reflection parameters, obeying the rules in the External input below. Its compressed image $j\circ\gamma$ has a continuous extension $\widehat\gamma$ to all of $\mathbb R$. The spatial projection, parametrized by physical time, is a complete generalized ray $x:\mathbb R\to\overline D$ and travels at unit speed. Curves obeying the same local rules on shorter intervals are ray segments; they are not independently quantified over in GCC or non-trapping. The complete-curve convention does not assert that every locally admissible segment has a complete extension.

For a time-independent open observation region $\omega\subset\overline D$ and $T>0$, $(\omega,T)$ satisfies the geometric control condition (GCC) when every complete generalized ray $x:\mathbb R\to\overline D$ has $x(t)\in\omega$ for some $t\in(0,T)$. GCC means that such a common $T$ exists. All complete admissible rays are quantified over; uniqueness at infinite-order contact is not asserted.

For a smooth compact obstacle $\Theta\subset\mathbb R^d$, its exterior is non-trapping when every complete generalized ray $x:\mathbb R\to\overline{\mathbb R^d\setminus\Theta}$ leaves every compact set in finite forward time, starting at $t=0$. This is a pointwise condition on complete rays, with no common escape time asserted here.

### External input

In the above boundary coordinates put $r_1=\partial_{y_n}r|_{y_n=0}$ and, for a function $f$ of canonical coordinates, $H_f=\sum_j(\partial_{\eta_j}f\,\partial_{y_j}-\partial_{y_j}f\,\partial_{\eta_j})$. On boundary tangential variables, $H_{r_0}$ uses the same formula with only tangential coordinates and momenta. Put $G^2=G$, the glancing set. For $k\ge0$, $G^{k+2}$ consists of glancing points where $H_{r_0}^j r_1=0$ for $0\le j<k$, and $G^\infty=\bigcap_{k\ge0}G^{k+2}$. For each integer $\ell\ge1$, the diffractive part of $G^{2\ell}\setminus G^{2\ell+1}$ is the subset where $H_{r_0}^{2\ell-2}r_1<0$; let $G_d$ be the union of these subsets.

The parameter domain is $\mathbb R\setminus B$, where every $s_0\in B$ is isolated in $B$; no reflection parameter is an endpoint of the complete curve. For $s\notin B$, $\gamma(s)$ is either an interior nonzero characteristic covector or a glancing characteristic covector, and $\gamma$ is differentiable there. It follows $H_p$ in the interior and at $G_d$, and $H_{r_0}$ at $G\setminus G_d$. For each $s_0\in B$ there is $\delta>0$ such that $\gamma(s)$ is interior for $s\in(s_0-\delta,s_0)\cup(s_0,s_0+\delta)$. The limits $\rho^\pm=\lim_{s\to s_0^\pm}\gamma(s)$ exist at the boundary, have the same base point and tangential covector, and have opposite nonzero normal covectors. Set $\widehat\gamma(s)=j(\gamma(s))$ for $s\notin B$ and $\widehat\gamma(s_0)=j(\rho^-)=j(\rho^+)$ for $s_0\in B$; the compressed curve $\widehat\gamma$ is continuous on $\mathbb R$.

The physical-time and spatial coordinates are thus defined also at reflections. The cited construction parametrizes the projected ray by physical time on all of $\mathbb R$, at unit speed. These rules include infinite-order glancing points; they do not assert that a point there determines a unique global curve. The boundaryless convention likewise uses complete unit-speed projected geodesics, without reflection.

The non-trapping convention in the Statement is the independent compact-obstacle convention of Burq, Gérard and Tzvetkov. The ray and GCC conventions are the local Euclidean specialization of the definitions of Le Rousseau, Lebeau, Terpolilli and Trélat. No observability theorem, global exterior existence or continuation theorem, or exterior propagation theorem is asserted by these definitions.

### Facts & Assumptions

The boundary-chart supplier fixes the smooth one-sided local graph convention; normal coordinates and compressed rays are supplied by the cited external construction. The bounded-domain supplier is used for that local chart convention, not to assert that an exterior domain is bounded.

The cotangent-bundle supplier identifies each cotangent space as the dual of its tangent space and the bundle as their disjoint union. The compressed bundle and its topology are additional imported structures.


## Continuous and mixed time-space function spaces

[Open item](item-def-se-foundation-time-function-spaces) · level 0


### Definition

Assume countable choice. Let $0<T\le\infty$ and let $X$ be a real or complex Banach space. When $T=\infty$, the interval $(0,T)$ means $(0,\infty)$. For finite $T$, write $C([0,T];X)$ for the functions $u:[0,T]\to X$ continuous in the norm metric, with $\|u\|_{C([0,T];X)}=\sup_{0\le t\le T}\|u(t)\|_X$.

For $1\le p\le\infty$, $L^p(0,T;X)$ is the set of almost-everywhere equivalence classes of strongly measurable maps $u:(0,T)\to X$ for which $\|u\|_{L^p(0,T;X)}$ is finite. Strong measurability means almost-everywhere norm convergence of measurable finite-range $X$-valued simple functions. For $p<\infty$ the norm is $(\int_0^T\|u(t)\|_X^p\,dt)^{1/p}$; for $p=\infty$ it is the essential supremum of $\|u(t)\|_X$. Time measure is restricted Lebesgue measure on $(0,T)$, including on $(0,\infty)$ when $T=\infty$. When $p=1$, integration of such a map over time means its Bochner integral.

Let $D\subset\mathbb R^n$ be Lebesgue measurable, with restricted Lebesgue measure, and let $1\le p,q\le\infty$. The spatial space $L^q(D;\mathbb C)$ is the usual quotient of measurable complex functions with finite $L^q$ norm by equality almost everywhere. For a jointly measurable complex function $u$ on the completed product $(0,T)\times D$, set $N_q(t)=\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{L^q(D)}$ for almost every $t$ for which the section is measurable, allowing $N_q(t)=\infty$, and put $N_q(t)=0$ on an exceptional null set. Define $L^p_tL^q_x=L^p(0,T;L^q(D))_{\mathrm{mixed}}$ as the product-almost-everywhere classes of such functions for which $\|N_q\|_{L^p(0,T)}<\infty$, with that quantity as norm. At either infinite exponent the corresponding norm is an essential supremum. This mixed notation alone does not require $t\mapsto u(t,\cdot)$ to be strongly measurable as an $L^q(D)$-valued map. The Bochner space $L^p(0,T;L^q(D))$ is used only when that additional requirement holds.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] A Banach space is a complete normed space over the stated scalar field; strong measurability is almost-everywhere norm approximation by measurable finite-range simple functions. [math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space) [math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)

[F2] A strongly measurable map is Bochner integrable exactly when its norm is integrable, and its integral is independent of the approximating sequence. [math:def-bochner-integrable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bochner-integrable-function) [math:thm-bochner-integrability-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bochner-integrability-criterion)

[F3] Scalar complex $L^q$ is the almost-everywhere quotient, and its displayed functional is a norm, including at $q=\infty$. Real scalar Minkowski holds for every exponent in $[1,\infty]$. [math:def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions) [math:thm-complex-holder-minkowski-and-the-quotient-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-holder-minkowski-and-the-quotient-norm) [math:thm-minkowski-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-minkowski-inequality-for-integrals)

[F4] For sigma-finite factors, completed-product Tonelli gives almost-everywhere measurable sections and measurable section integrals, and takes product-null sets to null sections almost everywhere. It assumes countable choice. [math:thm-tonelli-and-fubini-for-completed-product-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-tonelli-and-fubini-for-completed-product-measures) [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[F5] Under countable choice, complex $L^q$ is complete on every measure space for $1\le q\le\infty$; together with its quotient norm, it is therefore a complex Banach space. [math:thm-complex-lp-completeness-and-almost-everywhere-subsequences](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-lp-completeness-and-almost-everywhere-subsequences) [math:thm-complex-holder-minkowski-and-the-quotient-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-holder-minkowski-and-the-quotient-norm) [math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space) [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[F6] Every closed bounded real interval $[a,b]$ with $a\le b$ is compact. A continuous real-valued function on a compact subset of its real domain is bounded there. [math:thm-heine-borel-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-heine-borel-r) [math:cor-boundedness-theorem-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-boundedness-theorem-r)

[F7] Under countable choice, Lebesgue measure $\lambda_d$ is a complete measure on the Lebesgue sigma-algebra of $\mathbb R^d$. For finite $T$, the interval $(0,T)$ is Lebesgue measurable with $\lambda_1((0,T))=T$. The half-line $(0,\infty)=\bigcup_{k\ge1}(0,k)$ is also Lebesgue measurable. [math:def-lebesgue-measure-and-the-lebesgue-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-lebesgue-measure-and-the-lebesgue-sigma-algebra) [math:thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure) [math:thm-lebesgue-measure-of-a-box-of-every-kind](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-of-a-box-of-every-kind) [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[F8] Under countable choice, the measurable cubes $(-k,k]^d$, $k\ge1$, cover $\mathbb R^d$ and have finite Lebesgue measure. Every bounded Lebesgue measurable subset of $\mathbb R^d$ has finite measure. [math:prop-lebesgue-measure-is-sigma-finite-and-finite-on-bounded-sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-lebesgue-measure-is-sigma-finite-and-finite-on-bounded-sets) [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[F9] For two sigma-finite measure spaces under countable choice, the completed product is the completion of their product measure. [math:def-completed-product-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-completed-product-measure) [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[F10] The rationals are countably infinite; their canonical image in $\mathbb R$ is countable and dense. [math:thm-rationals-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-rationals-countable) [math:lem-q-and-irrationals-dense-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-q-and-irrationals-dense-r)

[F11] A countable union of measurable null sets is measurable and null by countable subadditivity. [math:thm-finite-and-countable-subadditivity-of-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-and-countable-subadditivity-of-measures)

[F12] A finite-valued complex function is measurable exactly when its real and imaginary parts are measurable. Real measurable functions have measurable positive and negative parts, and differences of finite real measurable functions are measurable. [math:def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions) [math:thm-arithmetic-and-lattice-operations-preserve-measurability](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-arithmetic-and-lattice-operations-preserve-measurability)

### Justification

**Given:** The measures, spaces, exponents and maps in the definition.

**Goal:** The displayed quantities are finite norms on the stated classes and the mixed norm is independent of representatives.

1.1 For a continuous path $u$, the norm triangle inequality gives $\|u(t)\|_X-\|u(s)\|_X\le\|u(t)-u(s)\|_X$; interchanging $s,t$ yields $|\|u(t)\|_X-\|u(s)\|_X|\le\|u(t)-u(s)\|_X$. Thus $t\mapsto\|u(t)\|_X$ is continuous. For finite $T>0$, F6 makes $[0,T]$ compact and this real function bounded there; the norm axioms and pointwise operations give the finite supremum norm. For strongly measurable maps, norms are measurable as limits of norms of simple approximants. Sums and scalar multiples are strongly measurable by adding and scaling approximants, and the pointwise norm inequality followed by scalar Minkowski gives the triangle inequality. Homogeneity is pointwise, and zero norm means the map is zero almost everywhere. For $p=1$, integrability and independence of the Bochner integral follow from F2. Applied to restricted Lebesgue measure on $D$, F5 shows that $L^q(D;\mathbb C)$ is Banach for every allowed $q$, including $q=\infty$, so it is an admissible target for the Bochner-space definition when the required strong measurability holds. [given, F1, F2, F3, F5, F6]

1.2 For a Lebesgue measurable set $E\subseteq\mathbb R^d$, the Lebesgue measurable subsets of $E$ form a sigma-algebra on $E$: relative complements are differences of Lebesgue measurable sets and countable unions remain Lebesgue measurable. Assign each such subset $A$ the value $\lambda_d(A)$. The empty-set and countable-additivity axioms follow directly from those of $\lambda_d$ in F7. If such an $A$ is null, every subset of $A$ is Lebesgue measurable by F7 and remains inside $E$, so this measure on $E$ is complete. This is the restricted Lebesgue measure used in the definition. For $E=D$ and $d=n$, the sets $D\cap(-k,k]^n$, $k\ge1$, are bounded Lebesgue measurable subsets, so F8 makes their measures finite; their union is $D$. For $E=(0,T)$ and $d=1$, F7 gives measurability for both finite $T$ and $T=\infty$. The sets $E\cap(-k,k]$, $k\ge1$, are bounded Lebesgue measurable sets of finite measure by F8 and cover $E$, so the time factor is sigma-finite even when $T=\infty$. Thus both restricted factors are complete and sigma-finite. F9 therefore supplies their completed product, and F4 applies with the existing countable-choice assumption. [given, F4, F7, F8, F9]

2.1 Since $u$ is finite-valued and complex measurable, F12 makes $(\operatorname{Re}u)^+$, $(\operatorname{Re}u)^-$, $(\operatorname{Im}u)^+$ and $(\operatorname{Im}u)^-$ nonnegative jointly measurable functions. Apply the nonnegative branch of F4 separately to these four functions and let $A_0$ be the union of their measurable null exceptional time sets. By F11, $A_0$ is measurable and null. Outside $A_0$, all four sections are measurable for the completed spatial factor, which is the restricted Lebesgue factor itself by step 1.2. Their values are finite, so their differences reconstruct measurable real and imaginary sections by F12; hence $u(t,\cdot)$ is complex measurable there. For finite $q$, apply F4 to $|u|^q$, enlarge its measurable null exceptional set by $A_0$, and set $N_q$ to zero there; taking the $q$th root of the resulting measurable section integral makes $N_q$ measurable. For $q=\infty$, identify rational thresholds with their canonical real images, as in F10. For each rational $r>0$, apply F4 to $\mathbf1_{\{|u|>r\}}$ to obtain a measurable section-integral function $I_r$ and a measurable null exceptional set $A_r$, with $I_r(t)=\lambda_n(\{x\in D:|u(t,x)|>r\})$ outside $A_r$. F10 and the assumed countable choice allow these exceptions to be chosen for all such $r$. By F11, $A=A_0\cup\bigcup_{r\in\mathbb Q,\ r>0}A_r$ is measurable and null. Set every $I_r$ and $N_\infty$ to zero on $A$; each $I_r$ remains measurable. Outside $A$, $N_\infty$ is the section essential supremum. [given, F4, F10, F11, F12, step 1.2]

3.1 For each real $a\ge0$, one has $\{t:N_\infty(t)>a\}=\bigcup_{r\in\mathbb Q,\ r>a}\{t:I_r(t)>0\}$. Indeed, outside $A$, if $N_\infty(t)>a$, density in F10 gives a rational $r$ with $a<r<N_\infty(t)$, also when $N_\infty(t)=\infty$; the section measure $I_r(t)$ must be positive, since otherwise $r$ would be an essential bound. Conversely, $I_r(t)>0$ means that no number below $r$ is an essential bound, so $N_\infty(t)\ge r>a$ by the infimum convention in F3. Both sides exclude $A$. The union is countable by F10 and consists of measurable sets by step 2.1. Negative superlevels are the whole time interval because $N_\infty\ge0$. Hence $N_\infty$ is measurable. If two representatives agree product-almost everywhere, F4 applied to their disagreement set makes their sections equal almost everywhere for almost every time, so F3 gives the same $N_q$ almost everywhere. [given, F3, F4, F10, step 1.2, step 2.1]

4.1 F3 gives the sectionwise triangle inequality, homogeneity and definiteness. Applying the scalar $L^p$ norm to the sectionwise bounds gives the mixed triangle inequality and homogeneity. If the mixed norm is zero, $N_q=0$ almost everywhere, so the sections vanish almost everywhere there; F4 then makes $u=0$ product-almost everywhere. The converse is immediate. Hence the mixed norm is well defined and definite on product-almost-everywhere classes. These arguments do not assert completeness or strong measurability into $L^\infty(D)$. [given, F3, F4, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 3.1] ∎


## Complex integer-order Sobolev notation

[Open item](item-def-se-foundation-integer-sobolev-notation) · level 0


### Definition

Assume Countable Choice: every sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. Let $D\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be open, with $n\ge1$, and let $k$ be a nonnegative integer. All functions below are complex-valued and identified when equal almost everywhere. For $1\le p\le\infty$, $W^{k,p}(D;\mathbb C)$ is the space of $L^p$ classes whose weak derivatives $D^\alpha u$ of every multi-index $\alpha\in\mathbb N_0^n$ with $|\alpha|\le k$ belong to $L^p(D;\mathbb C)$. A weak derivative is defined by integration against smooth compactly supported test functions, with the usual factor $(-1)^{|\alpha|}$; $D^0u=u$.

Write $H^k(D)=W^{k,2}(D;\mathbb C)$, with norm $\|u\|_{H^k(D)}=(\sum_{|\alpha|\le k}\|D^\alpha u\|_{L^2(D)}^2)^{1/2}$. Write $W^{k,\infty}(D)$ for $W^{k,\infty}(D;\mathbb C)$, equipped with $\|u\|_{W^{k,\infty}(D)}=\max_{|\alpha|\le k}\|D^\alpha u\|_{L^\infty(D)}$. In particular, the order-zero derivative requires $u$ itself to be essentially bounded.

Let $C_c^\infty(D;\mathbb C)$ denote the complex-valued smooth functions on $D$ whose supports are compact subsets of $D$. Define $H^k_0(D)=W^{k,2}_0(D;\mathbb C)$ as their closure in the $H^k(D)$ norm: $u\in H^k_0(D)$ exactly when, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there is $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(D;\mathbb C)$ with $\|u-\varphi\|_{H^k(D)}<\varepsilon$. This definition asserts no trace characterization.

### Definition references

[math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm)

[math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure)

### Justification

The complex Sobolev supplier defines the derivative classes and displayed finite-sum and maximum conventions under Countable Choice; the assumption is used in its representative-independence and weak-derivative uniqueness interface. [math:lem-sobolev-norm-is-well-defined-and-definite](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-sobolev-norm-is-well-defined-and-definite) establishes that these expressions are norms on the classes. [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure) supplies the inclusion of the test functions in the Sobolev space and the resulting metric closure, so the stated approximation condition defines a subset without requiring boundary traces.

### Remarks

Fractional or negative ordinary Sobolev spaces, Dirichlet spectral spaces, identifications between domains, norm equivalences and trace characterizations require separate statements.


## Bounded smooth fractional Dirichlet domain contract

[Open item](item-thm-se-bounded-fractional-dirichlet-input) · level 0


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $n\ge2$ and let $G\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a nonempty bounded open set with $C^\infty$ one-sided boundary charts. All functions are complex-valued. Let $B$ be the positive self-adjoint Dirichlet realization of $1-\Delta$ in $L^2(G)$, with domain $H^2(G)\cap H^1_0(G)$, and write $D(B^{s/2})$ for the spectral-power domain with its graph norm.

For $s\ge0$, $H^s(G)$ is the space of restrictions to $G$ of the whole-space Bessel-potential space $H^s(\mathbb R^n)$, equipped with the quotient norm. For integer $k$, this agrees with the usual weak $W^{k,2}(G)$ convention on this smooth bounded set. The space $H^1_0(G)$ is the $H^1(G)$-closure of $C_c^\infty(G)$. Write $d_G(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial G)$, and let $\gamma_0$ be the continuous boundary trace extending ordinary restriction wherever $s>1/2$.

For $0\le s\le2$, with equivalent norms,

$$D(B^{s/2})=\begin{cases}H^s(G),&0\le s<1/2,\\ \{u\in H^{1/2}(G):d_G^{-1/2}u\in L^2(G)\},&s=1/2,\\ \{u\in H^s(G):\gamma_0u=0\},&1/2<s\le2.\end{cases}$$

At the critical index the norm on the displayed space is $\|u\|_{H^{1/2}(G)}+\|d_G^{-1/2}u\|_{L^2(G)}$; at the other indices use the inherited $H^s(G)$ norm. In particular, no normal-derivative boundary condition is imposed at $s=3/2$. For $0<s<2$ this domain also equals $[L^2(G),H^2(G)\cap H^1_0(G)]_{s/2}$ with equivalent norms. At $s=0$ and $s=2$, the interpolation notation is understood to mean its respective endpoint.

Here $[X_0,X_1]_\theta$ is complex strip interpolation: use bounded continuous functions on the closed strip with values in $X_0+X_1$, holomorphic in its interior, whose boundary values belong continuously to the respective endpoint spaces and tend to zero at imaginary infinity; the norm is the infimum of the larger boundary supremum over functions taking the specified value at $\theta$.

### External input

Grubb, Section 2, equation (2.4), equation (2.5), Definition 2.1 and Theorem 2.2, supplies the bounded-domain power and complex-interpolation identifications. The application uses the Dirichlet boundary operator $\gamma_0$, exponent $p=2$, and the bijective realization of $1-\Delta$. Grubb explicitly gives the distance-weight formulation of the critical condition in the $L^2$ case. The spectral fractional operator here is the power of the Dirichlet realization, not the restricted fractional Laplacian. The cited source is not an exterior-domain theorem.

The integer zero-boundary notation is the closure in [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure); [math:thm-kernel-of-the-trace-is-w-one-p-zero](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-kernel-of-the-trace-is-w-one-p-zero) identifies it with the kernel of the $H^1$ trace on this bounded smooth domain. The whole-space fractional convention is that of [math:thm-fourier-characterisation-of-fractional-hilbert-sobolev-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fourier-characterisation-of-fractional-hilbert-sobolev-spaces), and spectral powers use [math:thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus). The choice assumption is retained from those published interfaces. The fractional-domain theorem is imported; the local argument below establishes the integer Sobolev identification and the bijectivity needed for its application, without reconstructing Seeley’s theorem.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[A1] The Axiom of Choice is assumed ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)); it supplies the Countable and Dependent Choice hypotheses of the published realization and functional-analysis inputs. The Sobolev, trace-kernel and spectral-power conventions are those recorded in the Statement and External input.

[F1] On the present bounded smooth set, [math:cor-dirichlet-laplacian-generates-an-analytic-heat-semigroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-dirichlet-laplacian-generates-an-analytic-heat-semigroup) (Statement (1), (3), and Proof step 4.1) supplies the self-adjoint operator $T=\Delta_D$ with $D(T)=H^2(G)\cap H^1_0(G)$, graph norm equivalent to $H^2(G)$, and $\langle Tu,u\rangle=-\|\nabla u\|_2^2$. Its proof establishes that $T$ is sectorial with vertex $0$ and angle $\pi/2$ in the $e^{tT}$ convention.

[F2] In this convention, the sectorial condition puts the positive real axis inside the resolvent set ([math:def-sectorial-operator-with-the-semigroup-sign-convention](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sectorial-operator-with-the-semigroup-sign-convention)). Membership $z\in\rho(T)$ means that $zI-T:D(T)\to L^2(G)$ is bijective with bounded inverse ([math:def-resolvent-and-spectrum-of-a-closed-unbounded-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-resolvent-and-spectrum-of-a-closed-unbounded-operator)).

[F3] The independent Grubb input recorded in External input gives the fractional-domain and interpolation identifications for a bijective smooth strongly elliptic Dirichlet realization. For the present application the differential expression is $1-\Delta$, the boundary operator is $\gamma_0$, the exponent is $p=2$, and the fractional power parameter is $a=s/2$.

[F4] For every $k\in\mathbb N_0$, [math:thm-fourier-characterisation-of-integer-order-hilbert-sobolev-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fourier-characterisation-of-integer-order-hilbert-sobolev-spaces) identifies $H^k(\mathbb R^n)$ with complex weak $W^{k,2}(\mathbb R^n)$, with equivalent norms and equality of norms at $k=0$, under Countable Choice. By [Bounded smooth domain Sobolev extension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-extension-theorem-for-bounded-smooth-domains), AC and the present bounded $C^\infty$ one-sided charts give, for each $k\ge1$, a bounded linear extension $E_k:W^{k,2}(G)\to W^{k,2}(\mathbb R^n)$ with $(E_ku)|_G=u$; take the ambient neighbourhood $V=\mathbb R^n$. At $k=0$ the same supplier supplies isometric zero extension. By [math:lem-bounded-restriction-and-cutoff-localisation-in-sobolev-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-bounded-restriction-and-cutoff-localisation-in-sobolev-spaces), restriction $W^{k,2}(\mathbb R^n)\to W^{k,2}(G)$ is a contraction for every $k\ge0$ under AC. These are mathematical inputs; no physical premise is used.

### Proof

**Given:** The same bounded smooth set and Dirichlet realization as in the Statement.

**Goal:** Establish the stated domain and interpolation identifications.

1.1 Fix $k\in\mathbb N_0$. The assumptions $n\ge2$, complex scalars and AC meet all the hypotheses in [F4]; for $k\ge1$, the boundary of $G$ is in particular $C^k$. Choose constants $a_k,b_k>0$ such that $\|v\|_{W^{k,2}(\mathbb R^n)}\le a_k\|v\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^n)}$ and $\|v\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^n)}\le b_k\|v\|_{W^{k,2}(\mathbb R^n)}$ under the whole-space identification. If $u\in H^k(G)$ and $U\in H^k(\mathbb R^n)$ restricts to $u$, restriction in [F4] yields $u\in W^{k,2}(G)$ and $\|u\|_{W^{k,2}(G)}\le a_k\|U\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^n)}$. Taking the infimum over all such $U$ gives $\|u\|_{W^{k,2}(G)}\le a_k\|u\|_{H^k(G)}$. [given, A1, F4, choose]

2.1 Conversely, for $u\in W^{k,2}(G)$ use the extension $E_k$ from [F4], with operator norm $M_k$, using zero extension when $k=0$. Then $E_ku\in H^k(\mathbb R^n)$ by the whole-space equivalence, $(E_ku)|_G=u$, and the quotient norm gives $\|u\|_{H^k(G)}\le\|E_ku\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^n)}\le b_kM_k\|u\|_{W^{k,2}(G)}$. Together with step 1.1 this proves $H^k(G)=W^{k,2}(G)$ with equivalent norms for every nonnegative integer $k$. At $k=0$ both whole-space comparison constants and the zero-extension norm can be taken to be one, so $H^0(G)=L^2(G)$ with equality of norms. At $k=1$ equivalent norms have the same closure of $C_c^\infty(G)$, hence the Statement's $H^1_0(G)$ equals weak $W^{1,2}_0(G)$ as defined in [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure). Thus the published realization and trace-kernel interfaces may be used with the restriction-space convention, in particular at orders one and two. [given, F4, step 1.1, algebra]

3.1 Apply [F1] to $G$. Its smooth boundary is in particular $C^2$, and the assumed Choice axiom covers its choice hypotheses. The integer identifications and the closure equality in step 2.1 identify the published weak Sobolev domain with the present convention. The given Dirichlet realization is $B=I-T$ on $D(T)=H^2(G)\cap H^1_0(G)$. Hence for $u\in D(B)$, $\langle Bu,u\rangle=\|u\|_2^2+\|\nabla u\|_2^2\ge\|u\|_2^2$, so $B\ge I$. Also $Tu=u-Bu$ and $Bu=u-Tu$ imply equivalence of the two graph norms, hence equivalence of the graph norm of $B$ and the $H^2(G)$ norm. [given, A1, F1, step 2.1, algebra]

4.1 Since $1$ lies on the positive real axis, the sectorial conclusion in [F1] and the conventions in [F2] give $1\in\rho(T)$. Thus $B=I-T:D(B)\to L^2(G)$ is bijective with bounded inverse $(I-T)^{-1}$. This proves the required onto assertion as well as injectivity; positivity alone was not used as a substitute for surjectivity. [F1, F2, step 3.1]

5.1 The expression $1-\Delta$ has smooth coefficients and is strongly elliptic, and $\gamma_0$ is its Dirichlet boundary operator. The bounded smooth domain, $p=2$, and bijectivity from step 4.1 therefore meet [F3]. With $a=s/2$, its imported theorem gives the displayed fractional spaces, their norms, and the complex-interpolation identity for $0<s<2$, including the distance-weight condition at $s=1/2$ and only the Dirichlet trace condition at $s=3/2$. At $s=0$ the power domain is $L^2(G)$; at $s=2$ it is $D(B)=H^2(G)\cap H^1_0(G)$ with the graph norm equivalence of step 3.1. Step 2.1 supplies the integer Sobolev and zero-boundary identifications used at these endpoints and by the trace-kernel interface. These are exactly the stated endpoint conventions. Thus all the original identifications follow from the imported theorem with its bijectivity hypothesis established locally. [given, F1, F3, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 4.1] ∎


## Distributional equation convention for complex Schrödinger solutions

[Open item](item-def-se-foundation-distributional-equation) · level 0


### Definition

Let $Q\subseteq\mathbb R^{1+n}$ be open, with $n\geq 1$; its first coordinate is time $t$ and the remaining coordinates are space $x=(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$. A test function on $Q$ is a complex-valued smooth function with compact support in $Q$; write $\mathcal D(Q)=C_c^\infty(Q;\mathbb C)$ and equip it with the test-function topology. A complex distribution on $Q$ is a continuous complex-linear functional on $\mathcal D(Q)$; its value on $\eta$ is written $\langle u,\eta\rangle$. The pairing is linear in both entries, with no conjugation of the test function. The space of these functionals is $\mathcal D'(Q)$.

For a multi-index $\alpha$ in the space-time coordinates, the distributional derivative is defined by $\langle\partial^\alpha u,\eta\rangle=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\langle u,\partial^\alpha\eta\rangle$. Here $|\alpha|$ is the sum of the entries of $\alpha$. The spatial Laplacian is $\Delta_x u=\sum_{j=1}^n\partial_{x_j}^2u$.

If $u,F\in\mathcal D'(Q)$, the equation $i\partial_tu+\Delta_xu=F$ holds **in distributions on $Q$** precisely when $\langle i\partial_tu+\Delta_xu,\eta\rangle=\langle F,\eta\rangle$ for every $\eta\in\mathcal D(Q)$. The same convention applies to an equation with additional terms, provided each term has independently been specified as a distribution. In particular, a nonlinear expression is included only when the chosen solution class makes it a distribution.

This interior space-time identity asserts neither existence of an initial value or boundary trace nor any regularity or local integrability of a nonlinear expression. Such properties and conditions require separate hypotheses. The bilinear distribution pairing is distinct from any conjugated Hilbert-space or antidual pairing.

### Definition references

[math:def-test-function-space-d-of-an-open-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-test-function-space-d-of-an-open-set)

[math:def-test-function-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-test-function-topology)

[math:def-distribution](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distribution)

[math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative)

### Justification

The test functions and their topology give the domain of the continuous linear functionals in [math:def-distribution](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distribution). By [math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative), each specified derivative is again a distribution; finite sums and scalar multiples of distributions are distributions. Consequently both sides of the displayed equation are functionals on the same test space, and equality on every test function is a well-defined equality in $\mathcal D'(Q)$. This construction does not establish that a proposed nonlinear expression defines a distribution.


## Weak convergence notation in normed state spaces

[Open item](item-def-se-foundation-weak-compactness-language) · level 0


### Definition

Let $X$ be a normed space over $\mathbb K=\mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$. Its bounded scalar-linear dual $X^*$ consists of the bounded $\mathbb K$-linear maps from $X$ to $\mathbb K$. For a sequence $(x_j)$ in $X$ and $x\in X$, write $x_j\rightharpoonup x$ in $X$, and say that $x_j$ converges weakly to $x$, if $\ell(x_j)\to\ell(x)$ in $\mathbb K$ for every $\ell\in X^*$. A weakly null sequence is one that converges weakly to $0$.

For a complex normed space, its realification $X_{\mathbb R}$ is the same additive group and norm with scalar multiplication restricted to real scalars. Weak convergence in $X_{\mathbb R}$ uses all bounded real-linear maps $X_{\mathbb R}\to\mathbb R$ in the preceding definition.

### Justification

The real and complex normed-space conventions are those of [math:def-norm-and-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-norm-and-normed-space) and [math:def-complex-norm-and-complex-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-norm-and-complex-normed-space), respectively.

The dual and weak topology are those of [math:def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space) and [math:def-weak-topology-on-a-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-weak-topology-on-a-normed-space). By [math:def-weak-convergence-of-nets-and-sequences](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-weak-convergence-of-nets-and-sequences), the displayed scalar-evaluation condition is exactly convergence of the sequence in the weak topology $\sigma(X,X^*)$: each evaluation is continuous, and finitely many eventual scalar conditions suffice for a basic weak neighborhood. By [math:lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm), the retained norm is a real norm on $X_{\mathbb R}$. The same argument applies to the real normed space $X_{\mathbb R}$. These conventions define convergence only; they assert no compactness or subsequence extraction.


## Helmholtz continuation through a harmonic lift

[Open item](item-lem-se-helmholtz-open-set-continuation) · level 0


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $D\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be connected and open, $n\ge2$, let $\mu\in\mathbb R$, and let $u\in H^1(D;\mathbb C)$ solve $\Delta u=\mu u$ weakly. Then $u$ has a real analytic representative in $D$. If $u$ vanishes almost everywhere on a nonempty open subset, then $u=0$ almost everywhere in $D$.

Here $H^1=W^{1,2}$ consists of $L^2$ classes with first weak derivatives in $L^2$. The weak equation means $-\int_D\nabla u\cdot\nabla\overline\varphi\,dx=\mu\int_Du\overline\varphi\,dx$ for every $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(D;\mathbb C)$. Real analytic means that near each point the representative has an absolutely convergent power series in the real coordinates, with complex coefficients. Connected means that the only relatively open and closed subsets of $D$ are $\varnothing$ and $D$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The Axiom of Choice, $D$, $n$, $\mu$ and $u$ as in the statement. In particular, Countable Choice follows from the Axiom of Choice. The Sobolev and weak-solution conventions are those in [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm), [math:def-hk-and-hk-zero-notation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hk-and-hk-zero-notation) and [math:def-local-weak-solution-for-a-divergence-form-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-local-weak-solution-for-a-divergence-form-operator). The conventions for connectedness and real analyticity are given by [math:def-connected-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-connected-space) and [math:def-real-analytic-germ-in-several-variables](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-analytic-germ-in-several-variables).

**Goal:** Obtain an analytic representative and prove its vanishing on $D$ when it vanishes on a nonempty open subset.

[F1] For the uniformly elliptic operator $-\Delta$, interior regularity gives $H^{k+2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ regularity of an $H^1$ weak solution whenever its right-hand side is in $H^k_{\mathrm{loc}}$; it requires Countable Choice ([math:thm-interior-h-k-plus-two-elliptic-regularity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-interior-h-k-plus-two-elliptic-regularity)).

[F2] Every bounded smooth ball is a $W^{k,2}$ extension domain, and on such a ball the higher-order Sobolev embedding gives a $C^m$ representative when $k>m+n/2$. These inputs require the Axiom of Choice ([Bounded $C^k$ domains admit integer-order Sobolev extension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-extension-theorem-for-bounded-smooth-domains), [math:thm-higher-order-sobolev-embedding](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-higher-order-sobolev-embedding)).

[F3] A real or complex harmonic function on an open subset of Euclidean space of dimension at least two is real analytic. A harmonic function on a connected open set which vanishes on a nonempty open subset vanishes everywhere. Both assertions require Countable Choice ([math:thm-harmonic-functions-are-real-analytic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-harmonic-functions-are-real-analytic), [math:cor-unique-continuation-for-harmonic-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-unique-continuation-for-harmonic-functions)).

[F4] A product of connected spaces is connected in the product topology ([math:thm-product-of-connected-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-product-of-connected-spaces)). The real line is order-convex, since a real number between two real numbers belongs to $\mathbb R$, so it is connected by [math:thm-connected-subsets-of-r-are-intervals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-connected-subsets-of-r-are-intervals). In particular it has no nontrivial clopen partition: the two pieces of such a partition would be nonempty disjoint closed subsets of $\mathbb R$, hence separated, contradicting that theorem. This is connectedness in the sense of [math:def-connected-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-connected-space).

[F5] For a real $C^2$ function $f$ on an open Euclidean set, $\Delta f$ is the sum of its second coordinate derivatives, and $f$ is harmonic if $\Delta f=0$ ([math:def-laplacian-of-a-c2-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-laplacian-of-a-c2-function)). For a complex function $f=f_1+if_2$, use $\Delta f=\Delta f_1+i\Delta f_2$; calling $f$ harmonic means that both real components are harmonic. Thus the classical convention applies separately to the real and imaginary parts.

[F6] Under Countable Choice, the componentwise classical derivatives through order $k$ of a complex $C^k$ function on an open Euclidean set represent its unique weak derivatives ([math:lem-classical-derivatives-are-weak-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-classical-derivatives-are-weak-derivatives)). We apply this with $k=2$ to the smooth representative; the stated Axiom of Choice supplies Countable Choice.

[F7] The real exponential is defined by its power series, which gives $\exp(0)=1$ ([math:def-real-exponential-function-and-e](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-exponential-function-and-e)). It is smooth with $\exp^{(m)}=\exp$ for every positive integer $m$ ([math:thm-derivative-of-exponential](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-derivative-of-exponential)). On $\mathbb R$, $\sin'=\cos$, $\cos'=-\sin$ and $\cos(0)=1$ ([math:thm-sine-and-cosine-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-sine-and-cosine-derivatives)); iterating these identities gives continuous derivatives of every order for sine and cosine.

[F8] The one-variable chain rule applies when the inner and outer functions are differentiable at the relevant limit points; here both domains are $\mathbb R$ ([math:thm-chain-rule](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-chain-rule)). Scalar multiples and products obey the usual differentiation rules ([math:thm-algebra-of-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-algebra-of-derivatives)). Finite componentwise products and compositions of $C^k$ Euclidean maps are $C^k$ for every finite $k$ ([math:thm-ck-euclidean-maps-closed-under-algebra-and-composition](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-ck-euclidean-maps-closed-under-algebra-and-composition)). These rules apply to real and imaginary components separately.

### Proof

**Given:** The Axiom of Choice, $D$, $n$, $\mu$ and $u$ as in the statement. In particular, Countable Choice follows from the Axiom of Choice. The Sobolev and weak-solution conventions are those in [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm), [math:def-hk-and-hk-zero-notation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hk-and-hk-zero-notation) and [math:def-local-weak-solution-for-a-divergence-form-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-local-weak-solution-for-a-divergence-form-operator). The conventions for connectedness and real analyticity are given by [math:def-connected-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-connected-space) and [math:def-real-analytic-germ-in-several-variables](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-analytic-germ-in-several-variables).

**Goal:** Obtain an analytic representative and prove its vanishing on $D$ when it vanishes on a nonempty open subset.

[F1] For the uniformly elliptic operator $-\Delta$, interior regularity gives $H^{k+2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ regularity of an $H^1$ weak solution whenever its right-hand side is in $H^k_{\mathrm{loc}}$; it requires Countable Choice ([math:thm-interior-h-k-plus-two-elliptic-regularity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-interior-h-k-plus-two-elliptic-regularity)).

[F2] Every bounded smooth ball is a $W^{k,2}$ extension domain, and on such a ball the higher-order Sobolev embedding gives a $C^m$ representative when $k>m+n/2$. These inputs require the Axiom of Choice ([Bounded $C^k$ domains admit integer-order Sobolev extension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-extension-theorem-for-bounded-smooth-domains), [math:thm-higher-order-sobolev-embedding](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-higher-order-sobolev-embedding)).

[F3] A real or complex harmonic function on an open subset of Euclidean space of dimension at least two is real analytic. A harmonic function on a connected open set which vanishes on a nonempty open subset vanishes everywhere. Both assertions require Countable Choice ([math:thm-harmonic-functions-are-real-analytic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-harmonic-functions-are-real-analytic), [math:cor-unique-continuation-for-harmonic-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-unique-continuation-for-harmonic-functions)).

[F4] A product of connected spaces is connected in the product topology ([math:thm-product-of-connected-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-product-of-connected-spaces)). The real line is order-convex, since a real number between two real numbers belongs to $\mathbb R$, so it is connected by [math:thm-connected-subsets-of-r-are-intervals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-connected-subsets-of-r-are-intervals). In particular it has no nontrivial clopen partition: the two pieces of such a partition would be nonempty disjoint closed subsets of $\mathbb R$, hence separated, contradicting that theorem. This is connectedness in the sense of [math:def-connected-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-connected-space).

[F5] For a real $C^2$ function $f$ on an open Euclidean set, $\Delta f$ is the sum of its second coordinate derivatives, and $f$ is harmonic if $\Delta f=0$ ([math:def-laplacian-of-a-c2-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-laplacian-of-a-c2-function)). For a complex function $f=f_1+if_2$, use $\Delta f=\Delta f_1+i\Delta f_2$; calling $f$ harmonic means that both real components are harmonic. Thus the classical convention applies separately to the real and imaginary parts.

[F6] Under Countable Choice, the componentwise classical derivatives through order $k$ of a complex $C^k$ function on an open Euclidean set represent its unique weak derivatives ([math:lem-classical-derivatives-are-weak-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-classical-derivatives-are-weak-derivatives)). We apply this with $k=2$ to the smooth representative; the stated Axiom of Choice supplies Countable Choice.

[F7] The real exponential is defined by its power series, which gives $\exp(0)=1$ ([math:def-real-exponential-function-and-e](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-exponential-function-and-e)). It is smooth with $\exp^{(m)}=\exp$ for every positive integer $m$ ([math:thm-derivative-of-exponential](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-derivative-of-exponential)). On $\mathbb R$, $\sin'=\cos$, $\cos'=-\sin$ and $\cos(0)=1$ ([math:thm-sine-and-cosine-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-sine-and-cosine-derivatives)); iterating these identities gives continuous derivatives of every order for sine and cosine.

[F8] The one-variable chain rule applies when the inner and outer functions are differentiable at the relevant limit points; here both domains are $\mathbb R$ ([math:thm-chain-rule](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-chain-rule)). Scalar multiples and products obey the usual differentiation rules ([math:thm-algebra-of-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-algebra-of-derivatives)). Finite componentwise products and compositions of $C^k$ Euclidean maps are $C^k$ for every finite $k$ ([math:thm-ck-euclidean-maps-closed-under-algebra-and-composition](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-ck-euclidean-maps-closed-under-algebra-and-composition)). These rules apply to real and imaginary components separately.



**Proof technique:** direct.

1.1 The equation is the weak equation $-\Delta u=-\mu u$. Starting from $u\in H^1(D)$, apply [F1] with $k=0$ to obtain $u\in H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$. If $u\in H^k_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, then $-\mu u\in H^k_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$, so [F1] gives $u\in H^{k+2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$. Induction and restriction to smaller open sets therefore give $u\in H^k_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)$ for every nonnegative integer $k$. The coefficients of $-\Delta$ are constant and satisfy all coefficient and ellipticity hypotheses in [F1]. [given, F1]

2.1 Around each point choose nested balls whose larger closure lies in $D$. For any $m\ge0$, choose an integer $k>m+n/2$; step 1.1 supplies $H^k$ regularity on the larger ball, and [F2] supplies a $C^m$ representative there. Representatives for different orders and overlapping balls agree almost everywhere, hence agree everywhere where both are continuous. They consequently define one $C^\infty$ representative on $D$. Its classical Laplacian equals $\mu u$ everywhere: by [F6] with $k=2$, the weak and classical derivatives agree for this complex smooth representative on the open set $D$, and the two continuous sides agree almost everywhere. [step 1.1, F2, F5, F6]

3.1 Define $a(t)=e^{\sqrt{-\mu}\,t}$ if $\mu<0$, $a(t)=1$ if $\mu=0$, and $a(t)=\cos(\sqrt\mu\,t)$ if $\mu>0$. In the first case, put $\lambda=\sqrt{-\mu}$: [F7] and the chain and scalar rules in [F8] give $a'=\lambda a$ and $a''=\lambda^2a=-\mu a$. In the third case, put $\lambda=\sqrt\mu$: the same rules give $a'=-\lambda\sin(\lambda t)$ and $a''=-\lambda^2\cos(\lambda t)=-\mu a$. For $\mu=0$ the constant function has $a''=0$. Thus in every case $a(0)=1$ by [F7], $a''=-\mu a$, and $a$ is smooth by [F7] and [F8]. Set $v(x,t)=a(t)u(x)$ on $D\times\mathbb R$, using the smooth representative from step 2.1. The coordinate projections are linear and smooth, so composition and multiplication in [F8], at every finite order, show that $v$ is smooth componentwise. By [F5] and the differentiation rules in [F8], its componentwise classical Laplacian is $\Delta_{x,t}v=a\Delta_xu+a''u=0$; taking real and imaginary parts shows that each part is harmonic, so $v$ is complex harmonic in the stated sense. By [F3], $v$ is real analytic in the $n+1$ real variables. Restricting its locally absolutely convergent power series at $(x,0)$ to $t=0$ shows that $u(x)=v(x,0)$ is real analytic on $D$. [step 2.1, F3, F5, F7, F8, algebra]

4.1 Suppose $u=0$ almost everywhere on a nonempty open $U\subseteq D$. Its continuous representative vanishes pointwise on $U$, so $v=0$ on the nonempty open set $U\times\mathbb R$. By [F4], $D\times\mathbb R$ is connected; it is open in $\mathbb R^{n+1}$. Harmonic continuation in [F3] gives $v=0$ there. Evaluating at $t=0$ gives $u=0$ on $D$, and hence the original Sobolev class is zero almost everywhere. [given, step 3.1, F3, F4] ∎


## Weak shifted Dirichlet form and energy bound on an open set

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-weak-dirichlet-form) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $U\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be nonempty and open, $H=L^2(U;\mathbb C)$, and $Q=H^1_0(U)$. The form $q(u,v)=\int_U(u\overline v+\nabla u\cdot\overline{\nabla v})$ is densely defined, closed and satisfies $q[u]\ge\|u\|_2^2$. Its self-adjoint representing operator $A\ge I$ has
$$D(A)=\{u\in H^1_0(U):\Delta u\in L^2(U)\},\qquad Au=u-\Delta u.$$
Here the Laplacian is distributional, and $\|u\|_{H^1}\le\|Au\|_2$ for $u\in D(A)$. Moreover $D(A^{1/2})=Q$ and $q[u]=\|A^{1/2}u\|_2^2$ on $Q$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice); its countable and dependent instances follow from [math:lem-ac-supplies-countable-and-dependent-choice-for-banach-integration](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ac-supplies-countable-and-dependent-choice-for-banach-integration). These supply the choice hypotheses of the Sobolev, Hilbert-space, distributional and form-representation inputs.

[F2] The weak derivatives and $H^1$ norm are those of [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm); $H^1_0(U)$ is the $H^1$ closure of $C_c^\infty(U)$ by [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure). Completeness is supplied by [math:thm-sobolev-spaces-are-banach-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-sobolev-spaces-are-banach-spaces), and density in $L^2$ by [math:lem-smooth-compactly-supported-functions-are-dense-in-ltwo-of-an-open-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-smooth-compactly-supported-functions-are-dense-in-ltwo-of-an-open-set).

[F3] [math:cor-dirichlet-laplacian-generates-an-analytic-heat-semigroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-dirichlet-laplacian-generates-an-analytic-heat-semigroup) gives the self-adjoint nonpositive form operator $\Delta_D$ on any nonempty open set; its weak domain is defined by the Dirichlet energy identity. Only clause (1) is used.

[F4] [physics:thm-nrqmx-closed-form-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/physics/item/thm-nrqmx-closed-form-representation) is a mathematics-domain supplier with inner products linear in the first variable. At $c=0$ its statement gives the self-adjoint representing operator, $D(A^{1/2})=Q$, and $q(u,v)=\langle Au,v\rangle$ for $u\in D(A)$ and $v\in Q$. The square-root form identity on all of $Q$ is deduced locally below from these stated interfaces and the spectral form-core input [F10].

[F5] By [math:lem-l-two-with-the-integral-pairing-is-a-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-l-two-with-the-integral-pairing-is-a-hilbert-space), $H=L^2(U;\mathbb C)$ is a complex Hilbert space for $\langle u,v\rangle=\int_Uu\overline v$, linear in the first variable, under Countable Choice supplied by [F1].

[F6] Distributional derivatives use the bilinear test pairing and signed-transpose convention of [math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative). By [math:rem-weak-derivatives-are-distributional-derivatives-with-function-values](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/rem-weak-derivatives-are-distributional-derivatives-with-function-values), weak derivatives agree with represented distributional derivatives under Countable Choice. Thus for $u\in H^1(U)$, $\Delta u=\sum_{j=1}^n\partial_j(\partial_ju)$ is a distribution, with $\langle\Delta u,\psi\rangle=-\sum_j\int_U\partial_ju\,\partial_j\psi$ for every test $\psi$. Here $\Delta u\in L^2$ means that this distribution is represented by an $L^2$ class.

[F7] Cauchy–Schwarz in a complex inner-product space is supplied by [math:thm-cauchy-schwarz-in-an-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-cauchy-schwarz-in-an-inner-product-space). Applying it to the $L^2$ pairings from [F5] and to the finite-dimensional coordinate pairing gives $|q(u,v)|\le\|u\|_{H^1}\|v\|_{H^1}$, and $|\langle h,v\rangle|\le\|h\|_2\|v\|_2\le\|h\|_2\|v\|_{H^1}$ for $h\in H$ and $u,v\in Q$.

[F8] For a nonnegative symmetric form, [math:def-closed-sectorial-form-and-its-associated-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-closed-sectorial-form-and-its-associated-operator) defines closedness by completeness in the shifted form norm $(q[u]+\|u\|_2^2)^{1/2}$, with $M=0$ and $\theta=0$ in its sector condition. Its form-domain boundedness and continuous inclusion requirements are checked below. Only the closedness definition is used; the positive representing-operator convention is that of [F4].

[F9] Under AC, [math:thm-spectral-theorem-for-unbounded-self-adjoint-operators](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-spectral-theorem-for-unbounded-self-adjoint-operators) supplies the spectral projection-valued measure $E$ of a self-adjoint operator on a nonzero complex Hilbert space. [math:thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus) supplies the Borel calculus, addition of constants and spectral support. By [math:lem-unbounded-pvm-integral-is-well-defined-and-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-unbounded-pvm-integral-is-well-defined-and-closed), $\|f(E)u\|^2=\int|f(t)|^2\,dE_u(t)$ on its squared-integrability domain, where $E_u(S)=\langle E(S)u,u\rangle$ and $E_u(\mathbb R)=\|u\|^2$. Square roots use the nonnegative square-root function on the spectral support. The zero Hilbert space has the unique zero operator and all these norm identities are zero.

[F10] Under AC, [math:lem-spectral-form-domain-and-core-of-a-semibounded-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-spectral-form-domain-and-core-of-a-semibounded-operator) applies to a self-adjoint operator $A\ge0$ on a complex Hilbert space. With its lower-bound parameter $c=0$, it states that $D(A)$ is dense in $D(A^{1/2})$ for the norm $(\|z\|_2^2+\|A^{1/2}z\|_2^2)^{1/2}$ and that $\langle Az,z\rangle=\|A^{1/2}z\|_2^2$ for $z\in D(A)$. This includes its zero-space convention. The density is in the spectral graph norm; its conversion into density in the given $H^1$ form norm is proved below.

### Proof

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

**Goal:** Prove the conclusions in the statement under its stated hypotheses.

1.1 By [F5], $H$ is the required complex Hilbert space. The form $q$ is symmetric sesquilinear and bounded on $Q$ by [F7], and $q[u]=\|u\|_{H^1}^2\ge\|u\|_2^2$. The space $Q$ is a closed subspace of the complete space $H^1(U)$, hence complete in $q[u]^{1/2}$. The shifted norm required by [F4] at $c=0$ satisfies $q[u]\le q[u]+\|u\|_2^2\le2q[u]$, so it is complete as well. The inclusion $Q\hookrightarrow H$ is continuous because $\|u\|_2\le\|u\|_{H^1}$, and the nonnegative real diagonal satisfies the sector condition with $M=0$ and $\theta=0$. Thus [F8], together with boundedness from [F7], identifies this completeness as closedness of $q$. The inclusion $Q\subseteq H$ is dense because $C_c^\infty(U)$ is dense in $L^2(U)$. With the choice hypotheses supplied by [F1], the closed-form theorem gives the self-adjoint representative. The weak identity identifies its domain and action with $I-\Delta_D$ from the any-open realization: $q(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle$ is equivalent to $\int_U\nabla u\cdot\overline{\nabla v}=\langle f-u,v\rangle$. Since $\Delta_D\le0$, this gives $A\ge I$. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F7, F8, given, algebra]

2.1 If $u\in D(A)$ and $Au=f$, test the form identity with $\phi=\overline\psi$ for $\psi\in C_c^\infty(U)$. By [F6], $\langle\Delta u,\psi\rangle=-\sum_j\int_U\partial_ju\,\partial_j\psi=\int_U(u-f)\psi$, so $\Delta u=u-f$ in distributions. Conversely if $u\in Q$ and $\Delta u=g\in L^2$, use $\psi=\overline\phi$ in this distributional identity to obtain $q(u,\phi)=\langle u-g,\phi\rangle$ for smooth compactly supported $\phi$. Both sides are continuous in the $H^1$ norm of $\phi$ by [F7], so the defining density of $Q$ extends the equality to every $\phi\in Q$. The form-operator domain characterization gives $u\in D(A)$ and $Au=u-g$. [F2, F4, F5, F6, F7, step 1.1]

2.2 If $H=\{0\}$ the square-root claims are immediate. Otherwise $A\ge I\ge0$ by step 1.1, so [F10] applies under [F1] with $c=0$. Its spectral square root is the one in [F9], and [F4] identifies $D(A^{1/2})=Q$. For every $z\in D(A)$, the representing identity and the operator-domain identity in [F10] give $q[z]=\langle Az,z\rangle=\|A^{1/2}z\|_2^2$. Thus the two forms agree on $D(A)$ before any extension to $Q$ is made. [F1, F4, F5, F9, F10, step 1.1]

3.1 For $u\in D(A)$ the form identity and Cauchy–Schwarz yield $\|u\|_{H^1}^2=\langle Au,u\rangle\le\|Au\|_2\|u\|_2\le\|Au\|_2\|u\|_{H^1}$. Division when $u\ne0$, with the zero case immediate, proves the bound. [F4, F5, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1, algebra]

4.1 Fix $u\in Q=D(A^{1/2})$. Choose, using [F1] and the density assertion in [F10], a sequence $u_k\in D(A)$ such that $\|u_k-u\|_2+\|A^{1/2}(u_k-u)\|_2\to0$. For differences in $D(A)$, step 2.2 gives $\|u_k-u_j\|_{H^1}^2=q[u_k-u_j]=\|A^{1/2}(u_k-u_j)\|_2^2\to0$ as $k,j\to\infty$. Completeness of $Q$ in the $H^1$ norm from step 1.1 yields $v\in Q$ with $\|u_k-v\|_{H^1}\to0$. Since $\|u-v\|_2\le\|u-u_k\|_2+\|u_k-v\|_{H^1}\to0$, this limit is $v=u$. Continuity of the two norms and step 2.2 now give $q[u]=\lim_k q[u_k]=\lim_k\|A^{1/2}u_k\|_2^2=\|A^{1/2}u\|_2^2$. This proves the full form-domain identity and also that $D(A)$ is dense in $Q$ for the original form norm. Expanding the identity at $u+v$ and $u+iv$ and comparing real and imaginary parts gives $q(u,v)=\langle A^{1/2}u,A^{1/2}v\rangle$ for all $u,v\in Q$. [F1, F2, F4, F7, F10, step 1.1, step 2.2, choose, algebra] ∎

### Source notes

The any-open realization, the closed-form definition and representation theorem, the spectral-calculus norm identity, and the spectral form-core lemma are used here. The full square-root form identity is proved by completion from the operator domain. No bounded-domain spatial regularity, spectral gap, compact resolvent or eigenfunction expansion is assumed. The density of tests in L² and Sobolev completeness supply the form-domain density and completeness requirements.


## Bounded collar regularity for an exterior weak Dirichlet function

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-bounded-collar-h2) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $\Omega=\mathbb R^n\setminus\Theta$, $n\ge2$, be open with $\Theta$ compact and smooth boundary. Here smooth boundary means that $\partial\Theta$ is a smooth embedded hypersurface without boundary: near each of its points, a rigid motion makes it the graph $s=b(y)$ of a smooth function on an open ball in $\mathbb R^{n-1}$. No assumption that $\Theta$ occupies one specified side is imposed. Let $B_L=\{x\in\mathbb R^n:|x|<L\}$, choose $L>0$ with $\Theta\Subset B_L$, and choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(B_L)$ equal to $1$ near $\Theta$. Put $G=B_L\setminus\Theta$ and write $H^k=W^{k,2}$ for $k=1,2$. If $u\in H^1_0(\Omega)$ and $\Delta u=g\in L^2(\Omega)$ in distributions, meaning $\int_\Omega u\Delta\phi=\int_\Omega g\phi$ for every $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\Omega)$, then $\eta u\in H^1_0(G)\cap H^2(G)$ and
$$\Delta(\eta u)=\eta g+2\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla u+(\Delta\eta)u,\qquad \|\eta u\|_{H^2(G)}\le C_{\eta,G}(\|g\|_{L^2(\Omega)}+\|u\|_{H^1(\Omega)}).$$

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice). Its countable instance supplies the choice hypotheses of the Sobolev, regular-distribution and regularity inputs below and permits a sequence of test-function approximants.

[F2] The Sobolev spaces and their weak derivatives use [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm), and $H^1_0$ is the norm closure of tests in [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure). All distribution pairings here are bilinear, without conjugation.

[F3] [math:lem-test-function-cutoffs-and-euclidean-localization](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-test-function-cutoffs-and-euclidean-localization) supplies the fixed obstacle cutoff and the auxiliary smooth cutoffs used to form a finite partition near the compact set $\overline G$. The cutoff and all its derivatives are bounded.

[F4] [math:lem-weak-leibniz-rule-with-a-smooth-factor](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-weak-leibniz-rule-with-a-smooth-factor) supplies the first-order identity $\partial_j(au)=a\partial_j u+(\partial_j a)u$ and the resulting $H^1$ multiplier bound for a smooth function with bounded first derivatives. Its second-order weak-derivative formula is not assumed for $u$.

[F5] [math:thm-global-h-two-dirichlet-regularity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-global-h-two-dirichlet-regularity) applies on a bounded one-sided $C^2$ domain $D$ to a weak solution $q\in H^1_0(D)$ of $-\Delta q=f\in L^2(D)$, giving $\|q\|_{H^2(D)}\le C_D(\|f\|_2+\|q\|_2)$. In [math:def-uniformly-elliptic-divergence-form-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-uniformly-elliptic-divergence-form-operator) this is the choice $a^{ij}=\delta_{ij}$, $b^i=c=0$, with ellipticity constant $1$ and constant principal coefficients. The homogeneous weak-solution convention is [math:def-weak-dirichlet-solution-for-a-divergence-form-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-weak-dirichlet-solution-for-a-divergence-form-operator); no nonzero trace data are used.

[F6] [math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative) defines $\Delta T=\sum_{j=1}^n\partial_j^2T$, and [math:def-locally-integrable-function-as-a-regular-distribution](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-integrable-function-as-a-regular-distribution) identifies $u,g$ with their regular distributions. Thus $\Delta u=g$ means precisely the test identity in the Statement, even if the individual second derivatives of $u$ are not function-valued.

[F7] [Bounded $C^k$ domains and boundary charts](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-c-k-domain-and-boundary-charts) gives the one-sided graph convention required for $D$ in the regularity theorem, and the flattening shear $(y,s)\mapsto(y,s-b(y))$. At the obstacle we use the graph part of this convention, allowing either or both graph sides to belong to $G$; the auxiliary domains constructed below each occupy just one side.

### Proof

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

**Goal:** Establish the distributional product formula and the bounded collar estimate.

1.1 Choose $u_j\in C_c^\infty(\Omega)$ tending to $u$ in $H^1(\Omega)$ and set $w=\eta u$ on $G$. Each $w_j=(\eta u_j)|_G$ belongs to $C_c^\infty(G)$: its support misses $\Theta$ because the support of $u_j$ does, and misses $\partial B_L$ because the support of $\eta$ does. The first-order multiplier bound gives $w_j\to w$ in $H^1(G)$ and $\|w\|_{H^1(G)}\le C_\eta\|u\|_{H^1(\Omega)}$. Hence $w\in H^1_0(G)$. [F1, F2, F3, F4]

1.2 The boundary of $G$ is $\partial\Theta\cup\partial B_L$, with these two compact sets separated by a positive distance. In a sufficiently small cylindrical graph chart at $\partial\Theta$, the two open sides are connected and contain no boundary point of $\Theta$. Each side therefore lies entirely in $\operatorname{int}\Theta$ or entirely in $\Omega$: these two disjoint open sets partition the complement of $\partial\Theta$. At least one side lies in $\Omega$, and both may do so. At $\partial B_L$, exactly the inner side belongs to $G$. It suffices to obtain the regularity estimate separately on each occupied local side; we make no assertion about how these sides join in a global component. [F7, given]

2.1 For $\phi\in C_c^\infty(G)$, extend $\phi$ by zero as a test on $\Omega$ and apply $\Delta u=g$ to $\eta\phi$. Expanding derivatives of this smooth test gives $\int_G w\Delta\phi=\int_G\eta g\phi-2\int_G u\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\phi-\int_G u(\Delta\eta)\phi$. For each coordinate, the first weak-derivative identity for $u$ tested against $(\partial_j\eta)\phi$ gives $-\int_Gu(\partial_j\eta)\partial_j\phi=\int_G((\partial_j u)(\partial_j\eta)+u\partial_j^2\eta)\phi$. Summing yields $\int_G w\Delta\phi=\int_G h\phi$, where $h=\eta g+2\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla u+(\Delta\eta)u$. Thus $\Delta w=h$ in distributions and $\|h\|_{L^2(G)}\le C_\eta(\|g\|_{L^2(\Omega)}+\|u\|_{H^1(\Omega)})$. Only first weak derivatives of $u$ have been used. [F2, F3, F6, step 1.1, algebra]

2.2 Here is a bounded smooth auxiliary domain on one side of a graph, agreeing with that side near its central boundary point. In flattened coordinates take the unit ball centered at $-e_n$, whose upper boundary is $t=a(y)=-1+\sqrt{1-|y|^2}$. Choose $0\le\chi\le1$ smooth, supported in $|y|<1/2$ and equal to $1$ for $|y|\le1/4$. Extend $\chi a$ by zero, and apply the global shear $T(y,t)=(y,t-\chi(y)a(y))$ to the ball. Its inverse is $(y,t)\mapsto(y,t+\chi(y)a(y))$, so its image $D_*$ is bounded with smooth boundary. Its upper boundary is $(1-\chi)a\le0$, and near the origin it is exactly $t=0$; hence $D_*\subset\{t<0\}$ and agrees with that half-space near the origin. Dilate it sufficiently far into the given chart, then apply the inverse flattening shear and the rigid motion from that chart. This gives a bounded smooth domain $D\subset G$ on the chosen side, agreeing with that side in a smaller ambient neighbourhood $V$ of the boundary point. For the other side first reflect $t$. The dilated closure stays inside the chart, so every part of $\partial D$ has the one-sided $C^2$ charts required by the regularity supplier. The part of $\partial D$ inside $V$ is the original graph; the remaining, artificial boundary misses a still smaller neighbourhood of the point. [F3, F7, step 1.2, construct]

3.1 Take such smaller neighbourhoods $V$ at boundary points, with a domain $D$ for each occupied side, and interior neighbourhoods compactly contained in balls $D\Subset G$. A finite number cover $\overline G$. They admit smooth cutoffs $\zeta_i$ supported compactly in their respective neighbourhoods, with $\sum_i\zeta_i=1$ near $\overline G$: choose finitely many cutoffs $\rho_i$ supported in these neighbourhoods whose sum $S$ is positive near $\overline G$, choose a compactly supported cutoff $\tau$ equal to $1$ near $\overline G$ with support in $\{S>0\}$, and set $\zeta_i=\tau\rho_i/S$ there and zero elsewhere. The $\rho_i$ can be obtained by first taking smaller open sets covering $\overline G$ and then applying the cutoff input to their compact closures. For each boundary index, shrink the neighbourhood before choosing the cutoffs so that every corresponding $D$ agrees with its occupied side there and its artificial boundary misses $\operatorname{supp}\zeta_i$. This cover and all its constants depend only on the fixed geometry. [F3, step 1.2, step 2.2, construct]

4.1 For every interior index or occupied boundary side set $q=(\zeta_i w)|_D$. The functions $(\zeta_i w_j)|_D$ are in $C_c^\infty(D)$: the artificial boundary misses their supports, and $w_j$ misses the original boundary. They converge to $q$ in $H^1(D)$, so $q\in H^1_0(D)$. Repeating the test computation of step 2.1, now with $w$, $h$ and $\zeta_i$, gives $\Delta q=h_i:=\zeta_i h+2\nabla\zeta_i\cdot\nabla w+(\Delta\zeta_i)w$ in $D$, with $\|h_i\|_{L^2(D)}\le C_i(\|h\|_{L^2(G)}+\|w\|_{H^1(G)})$. This says $\int_D\nabla q\cdot\overline{\nabla v}=-\int_D h_i\overline v$ first for smooth compactly supported $v$, and then for every $v\in H^1_0(D)$ by its defining density and the $L^2$ bounds. Thus $q$ satisfies exactly the homogeneous weak Dirichlet problem for $-\Delta$ with forcing $-h_i$. [F2, F4, F5, F6, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 Apply the bounded-domain theorem on each of the finitely many domains $D$ to obtain $\|q\|_{H^2(D)}\le C_i(\|h\|_{L^2(G)}+\|w\|_{H^1(G)})$. Regard each $q$ as a function on $G$ by assigning zero off its chosen local side and off its supporting neighbourhood. This extension is $H^2(G)$: it vanishes in a neighbourhood of every artificial boundary, and its remaining boundary is contained in $\partial G$, which no compactly supported test in $G$ meets. Applying the weak derivative identities on these open pieces, and a finite smooth partition of a compact test support, therefore gives the weak derivatives on $G$ as the extensions of those on $D$, with the same norms. The sum of these extended functions is $\sum_i\zeta_i w=w$; when both sides of one chart are occupied, their two functions occupy disjoint parts of $G$. The finite sum belongs to $H^2(G)$ and is bounded there by $C_G(\|h\|_{L^2(G)}+\|w\|_{H^1(G)})$. Substituting steps 1.1 and 2.1 gives the stated estimate, and step 2.1 already gives the displayed Laplacian formula. This argument includes a thin smooth obstacle with both sides occupied and also the case $\Theta=\varnothing$. [F2, F3, F5, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 4.1] ∎



## Whole-space H² regularity from an L² distributional Laplacian

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-whole-space-h2) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. If $v\in L^2(\mathbb R^n)$ and $\Delta v=h\in L^2(\mathbb R^n)$ distributionally, then $v\in H^2(\mathbb R^n)$ and
$$\|v\|_{H^2}\le C_n(\|v\|_2+\|h\|_2).$$
The Fourier transform uses $\widehat{\partial_jv}=2\pi i\xi_j\widehat v$; the weak-derivative $H^2$ norm and the weight $(1+4\pi^2|\xi|^2)$ norm are equivalent, rather than identical.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice); its countable instances supply the choice hypotheses inherited from the Fourier, Sobolev and regular-distribution interfaces below. No additional choice is used in the local computation.

[F2] [math:lem-weak-derivatives-are-polynomial-fourier-multipliers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-weak-derivatives-are-polynomial-fourier-multipliers) gives $\mathcal F(D^\alpha u)=(2\pi i\xi)^\alpha\mathcal Fu$ for $u\in\mathcal S'(\mathbb R^n)$. Summing the pure second derivatives gives $\mathcal F(\Delta u)=-4\pi^2|\xi|^2\mathcal Fu$. This use does not assume that the individual second derivatives belong to $L^2$.

[F3] [math:thm-plancherel](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-plancherel) gives a unitary transform $\mathcal F_2:L^2\to L^2$, with $\|\mathcal F_2 f\|_2=\|f\|_2$.

[F4] Every $L^2$ class $f$ defines the regular tempered distribution $u_f(\varphi)=\int f\varphi$ by [math:thm-polynomial-growth-functions-define-tempered-distributions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-polynomial-growth-functions-define-tempered-distributions), and $\mathcal F u_f=u_{\mathcal F_2 f}$ in $\mathcal S'$ by [math:thm-fourier-transform-agrees-with-l-one-and-plancherel-transforms](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fourier-transform-agrees-with-l-one-and-plancherel-transforms).

[F5] [math:lem-smooth-polynomially-bounded-multipliers-on-schwartz-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-smooth-polynomially-bounded-multipliers-on-schwartz-space) defines multiplication by a smooth symbol with polynomially bounded derivatives through $\langle au,\varphi\rangle=\langle u,a\varphi\rangle$. In particular, for a polynomial $a$ and an $L^2$ class $f$, this pairing is $\int af\varphi$, so $a u_f=u_{af}$; the product $af$ is locally integrable because $a$ is bounded on each compact set.

[F6] [math:thm-locally-integrable-functions-embed-in-distributions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-locally-integrable-functions-embed-in-distributions) makes the regular-distribution map injective modulo almost-everywhere equality on $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Thus equality of regular distributions implies equality of their locally integrable representatives almost everywhere.

[F7] [math:thm-bessel-potential-space-has-the-weighted-tempered-distribution-characterisation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bessel-potential-space-has-the-weighted-tempered-distribution-characterisation) states that $u\in\mathcal S'$ belongs to the canonical image of $H^s$ if and only if $\langle\xi\rangle^s\mathcal Fu=u_g$ for some $g\in L^2$, with $\|E_s^{-1}u\|_{H^s}=\|g\|_2$. Here $\langle\xi\rangle=(1+|\xi|^2)^{1/2}$; the application below is at $s=2$.

[F8] [math:thm-fourier-characterisation-of-integer-order-hilbert-sobolev-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fourier-characterisation-of-integer-order-hilbert-sobolev-spaces) identifies the canonical image of $H^2$ with the regular distributions of $W^{2,2}$ classes, and compares the weak-derivative norm with $\|\langle\xi\rangle^2\mathcal F_2 f\|_2$ and $\|(1+4\pi^2|\xi|^2)\mathcal F_2 f\|_2$, with constants depending only on $n$.

[F9] [math:thm-tempered-distributions-embed-continuously-in-distributions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-tempered-distributions-embed-continuously-in-distributions) states that restriction from $\mathcal S'$ to compactly supported tests is injective. Thus equality of two tempered distributions on compactly supported tests is equality in $\mathcal S'$.

### Proof

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

**Goal:** Prove the stated regularity and norm estimate.

1.1 By F4, $v,h$ define tempered distributions $u_v,u_h$. The distributional hypothesis gives $\Delta u_v=u_h$ on compactly supported tests. The left side is tempered by F2, so F9 gives the same equality in $\mathcal S'$. Write $\widehat v=\mathcal F_2v$ and $\widehat h=\mathcal F_2h$. Applying F2, F4 and F5 yields $u_{\widehat h}=-4\pi^2|\xi|^2u_{\widehat v}=u_{-4\pi^2|\xi|^2\widehat v}$ in $\mathcal S'$. [F1, F2, F4, F5, F9, given]

2.1 By F3, $\widehat v,\widehat h\in L^2$. The polynomial multiple $-4\pi^2|\xi|^2\widehat v$ is locally integrable, as in F5, so restriction of step 1.1 to compactly supported tests and F6 give $\widehat h=-4\pi^2|\xi|^2\widehat v$ almost everywhere. In particular $|\xi|^2\widehat v\in L^2$, and F3 gives $\||\xi|^2\widehat v\|_2=(4\pi^2)^{-1}\|h\|_2$. [F3, F5, F6, step 1.1]

3.1 Step 2.1 and F3 give $\|(1+4\pi^2|\xi|^2)\widehat v\|_2\le\|v\|_2+\|h\|_2$. Since $1+|\xi|^2\le1+4\pi^2|\xi|^2$, the class $g=\langle\xi\rangle^2\widehat v$ lies in $L^2$ and satisfies $\|g\|_2\le\|v\|_2+\|h\|_2$. By F4 and F5, $\langle\xi\rangle^2\mathcal Fu_v=u_g$, where the multiplier is the polynomial $1+|\xi|^2$. Applying F7 at $s=2$ therefore gives $u_v=E_2U$ for a unique $U\in H^2$ with $\|U\|_{H^2}=\|g\|_2$. [F3, F4, F5, F7, step 2.1, algebra]

4.1 By F8, the distribution $E_2U=u_v$ is $u_f$ for a class $f\in W^{2,2}$. F6 implies $f=v$ almost everywhere, so $v$ has all weak derivatives through order two in $L^2$. The norm comparison in F8 and step 3.1 give $\|v\|_{W^{2,2}}\le C_n\|g\|_2\le C_n(\|v\|_2+\|h\|_2)$; the Bessel-potential norm of the same class is already bounded in step 3.1. Hence the asserted $H^2$ regularity and estimate hold with either stated equivalent norm. [F6, F8, step 3.1] ∎



## Spectral cutoff cores and unitary identifications of completed scales

[Open item](item-lem-author-03489e9088b7-spectral-cutoff-scale-identifications) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $A\ge I$ be self-adjoint on a nonzero complex Hilbert space $H$, with inner product linear in the first variable and spectral measure $E$. Write $\mathcal C=D(A^\infty)=\bigcap_{n\ge1}D(A^n)$, and let $H_A^s$ be the Hilbert completion of $\mathcal C$ for $\|u\|_s=\|A^{s/2}u\|_H$, for $s\in\mathbb R$. Then $E_N=E([1,N])$ has range in $\mathcal C$ and converges to the identity at every scale level. The map $J_su=A^{s/2}u$ extends to a unitary $J_s:H_A^s\to H$. For $s\ge0$, the canonical embedding in $H$ identifies $H_A^s$ with $D(A^{s/2})$ with exactly this norm. The core powers extend to onto isometries $A^{-r/2}:H_A^s\to H_A^{s+r}$ for every real $r,s$.

The scales have canonical compatible embeddings: if $b\ge a$, the identity on $\mathcal C$ extends to a contractive injective map $H_A^b\to H_A^a$. Under $J_a$ this couple is exactly $(H,D(A^{(b-a)/2}))$, with the second norm $\|A^{(b-a)/2}v\|_H$. Any finite set of scale levels may therefore be placed in the common Hausdorff Hilbert space at their smallest index.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The spectral theorem gives the PVM of a self-adjoint operator under AC [math:thm-spectral-theorem-for-unbounded-self-adjoint-operators](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-spectral-theorem-for-unbounded-self-adjoint-operators).

[F2] The Borel calculus gives exact domains, closedness, products and the norm integral, including spectral truncation convergence [math:thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus).
