[F3] AC is assumed for the spectral suppliers. Restricting AC to countable families supplies the countable-choice assumptions of the completion and extension suppliers [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice).

[F4] For a self-adjoint operator with $\langle Au,u\rangle\ge c\|u\|^2$ on $D(A)$, its spectral PVM is carried on $[c,\infty)$ [math:lem-spectral-form-domain-and-core-of-a-semibounded-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-spectral-form-domain-and-core-of-a-semibounded-operator).

[F5] A normed-space completion has a dense linear isometric embedding and is Banach; under countable choice such a completion exists with the compatible norm and vector-space structure [math:def-completion-of-a-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-completion-of-a-normed-space) [math:thm-metric-completion-carries-a-unique-banach-space-structure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-completion-carries-a-unique-banach-space-structure).

[F6] Under countable choice, the norm completion of a real or complex inner-product space has a unique extending inner product inducing its completion norm and is Hilbert [math:thm-completion-of-an-inner-product-space-is-hilbert](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-completion-of-an-inner-product-space-is-hilbert).

[F7] Under countable choice, a bounded linear map from a dense normed subspace into a Banach space extends uniquely to the ambient normed space, with the same operator norm [math:thm-extension-of-a-bounded-map-from-a-dense-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-extension-of-a-bounded-map-from-a-dense-subspace).

### Proof

**Given:** The data and AC in the statement.

**Goal:** All asserted identifications and bounds, with the stated exact norms.

1.1 Interpret $A\ge I$ as $\langle Au,u\rangle\ge\|u\|_H^2$ for $u\in D(A)$. Apply [F4] with $c=1$: $E(( -\infty,1))=0$. Thus all scalar spectral measures are carried on $[1,\infty)$. For every real $t$, define $A^t$ by the Borel multiplier $\lambda^t$ there, extended by $1$ below $1$; values below $1$ do not affect the operator. For $u\in\mathcal C$ every such power is defined, since an integer $m\ge1$ with $2m\ge2t$ gives $\lambda^{2t}\le\lambda^{2m}$ on this carrier. [given, F1, F2, F3, F4]

2.1 For $u\in H$ and every positive integer $k$, the norm and product formulas give $\|A^kE_Nu\|_H^2=\int_{[1,N]}\lambda^{2k}\,dE_u\le N^{2k}\|u\|_H^2$, so $E_NH\subset\mathcal C$. Also $\|(I-E_N)u\|_H^2=E_u((N,\infty))\to0$, proving density of $\mathcal C$ in $H$. For $u\in\mathcal C$ and real $s$, the integrable measure $\lambda^s\,dE_u$ has tails tending to zero, so $\|(I-E_N)u\|_s^2=\int_{(N,\infty)}\lambda^s\,dE_u\to0$. The same formulas give $\|E_Nu\|_s\le\|u\|_s$. [F1, F2, step 1.1]

3.1 The linear space $\mathcal C$ has the first-linear Hermitian pairing $\langle u,v\rangle_s=\langle A^{s/2}u,A^{s/2}v\rangle_H$. It is positive definite: if its diagonal is zero, $\int_{[1,\infty)}\lambda^s\,dE_u=0$; positivity of $\lambda^s$ implies $E_u([1,\infty))=0$, hence $u=0$. Its norm is exactly $\|u\|_s$. Therefore [F5] and [F6], with countable choice from [F3], construct the Hilbert completion $H_A^s$ with dense embedded core, which we identify with $\mathcal C$. Apply [F7] to each core contraction $E_N:\mathcal C\to H_A^s$. For $x\in H_A^s$ and $u\in\mathcal C$, the extension satisfies $\|(I-E_N)x\|_s\le2\|x-u\|_s+\|(I-E_N)u\|_s$. First approximate $x$ by $u$, then use step 2.1; this proves strong convergence on every completed scale. [F2, F3, F5, F6, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 The map $J_s:\mathcal C\to H$, $J_su=A^{s/2}u$, is linear and isometric. By [F7] it extends to $H_A^s$, and passage to limits preserves the norm and pairing. Its range is closed: if $J_sx_n$ converges in $H$, isometry makes $x_n$ Cauchy; completeness gives $x_n\to x$ and its image converges to $J_sx$. Its range contains $E_NH$: if $v=E_Nv$, then $u=A^{-s/2}v$ has spectral support in $[1,N]$, lies in $\mathcal C$, and $J_su=v$ by the product formula. Step 2.1 makes the union of these cutoff ranges dense in $H$, so $J_s$ is onto and unitary. The core identity $J_sE_N=E_NJ_s$ extends by continuity; since $A^{-s/2}E_NH\subset\mathcal C$, it also identifies every completed cutoff range with core vectors. [F2, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 3.1]

4.2 For any real $r$ and $u\in\mathcal C$, $A^{-r/2}u$ belongs to $\mathcal C$: for each integer $k\ge0$, choose an integer $m\ge1$ with $2m\ge2k-r$; then the relevant moment $\int_{[1,\infty)}\lambda^{2k-r}\,dE_u$ is bounded by $\int_{[1,\infty)}\lambda^{2m}\,dE_u<\infty$. The same argument with $-r$ shows that $A^{r/2}$ preserves $\mathcal C$, and the product formula makes these core maps inverse. Moreover, $\|A^{-r/2}u\|_{s+r}^2=\int_{[1,\infty)}\lambda^s\,dE_u=\|u\|_s^2$. Extend both core maps using [F7] between the Hilbert completions. Norm equality passes to limits, and their compositions are identities by density and uniqueness of extension. They are thus inverse onto isometries at all real indices. [F2, F7, step 1.1, step 3.1]

5.1 For $s\ge0$, $A^{-s/2}$ is a bounded contraction on $H$ and is injective, since its multiplier is strictly positive on the spectral carrier. The map $\iota_s=A^{-s/2}J_s:H_A^s\to H$ is therefore a contractive injection and equals the identity on $\mathcal C$, so it is the canonical extension of that identity. The exact product-domain formula gives $\operatorname{ran}A^{-s/2}=D(A^{s/2})$: every $v\in H$ has $A^{-s/2}v\in D(A^{s/2})$ and $A^{s/2}A^{-s/2}v=v$, while every $u\in D(A^{s/2})$ satisfies $u=A^{-s/2}A^{s/2}u$. Surjectivity of $J_s$ gives the asserted range of $\iota_s$, and $\|A^{s/2}\iota_s x\|_H=\|J_sx\|_H=\|x\|_s$. Spectral truncations converge in this exact domain norm by its integrable spectral tails, so no graph-norm factor is introduced. [F2, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 4.1]

6.1 If $b\ge a$, then $\lambda^a\le\lambda^b$ on $[1,\infty)$, so the identity on $\mathcal C$ extends by [F7] to a contraction $i_{b,a}:H_A^b\to H_A^a$. On the core, and hence on the completions, $J_ai_{b,a}=A^{(a-b)/2}J_b$. The multiplier $A^{(a-b)/2}$ is injective and has range $D(A^{(b-a)/2})$ by the inverse-product argument of step 5.1. Therefore $i_{b,a}$ is injective, and for $v=J_ai_{b,a}x$ one has $\|A^{(b-a)/2}v\|_H=\|J_bx\|_H=\|x\|_b$. This proves precisely the claimed couple and its second norm. For $c\ge b\ge a$, $i_{b,a}i_{c,b}=i_{c,a}$ because both maps agree on the dense core; consequently a finite collection embeds compatibly in the Hilbert space at its minimum index, which is Hausdorff. [F2, F7, step 1.1, step 3.1, step 4.1, step 5.1] ∎


## Compatible compact Banach triples

[Open item](item-def-author-ab209e1f475e-compact-banach-triple) · level 0


### Definition

Let $X,B,Y$ be real Banach spaces, or complex Banach spaces over the same field, in the sense of [math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space). A **compatible compact Banach triple** is a pair of injective linear maps $j:X\to B$ and $k:B\to Y$ such that $j$ is compact in the sense of [math:def-compact-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-linear-operator) and $k$ is bounded. We identify $X$ with $j(X)$ and $B$ with $k(B)$, using $k\circ j$ for the map from $X$ into $Y$; the notation is $X\Subset B\hookrightarrow Y$. Bounded means that there are finite constants $C_j,C_k$ with $\|jx\|_B\le C_j\|x\|_X$ and $\|kb\|_Y\le C_k\|b\|_B$. No density, reflexivity or Hilbert pivot is part of this definition. Compact means that the image of every norm-bounded subset of $X$ has compact closure in $B$.

### Justification

**Given:** The spaces and maps in the Definition.

1.1 A compact linear map is bounded by the final assertion of [math:def-compact-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-linear-operator), so $C_j$ exists. The composite is injective because both maps are injective, and $\|kjx\|_Y\le C_k C_j\|x\|_X$. Thus the three spaces can consistently be identified along these maps. [given, algebra]

1.2 Completeness is already required of each space by [math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space). The definition adds specified embeddings and compactness; it does not assert existence of such maps for arbitrary Banach spaces. Therefore its notation has exactly the stated hypotheses and no implicit Hilbert-space assumption. [given] ∎


## Bochner spaces and bounded inclusion of value spaces

[Open item](item-lem-author-ab209e1f475e-bochner-lp-inclusion) · level 0


### Statement

Assume Countable Choice ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)). For a Banach space $E$ ([math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space)), $0<T<\infty$ and $1\le p<\infty$, equip $(0,T)$ with Lebesgue measure and define $L^p(0,T;E)$ to be the almost-everywhere equivalence classes of strongly measurable functions ([math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)) whose norm has finite scalar $L^p$ integral; the equivalence convention is [math:def-l-p-space-as-a-quotient-by-null-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-l-p-space-as-a-quotient-by-null-functions). Its norm is $\|u\|_{L^p(E)}=(\int_0^T\|u(t)\|_E^p\,dt)^{1/p}$. This is Banach by [Bochner $L^p$ is complete for a Banach value space](https://alphabetamath.cc/physics/item/thm-pe12-bochner-lp-completeness) for real $E$ and by realification [math:lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm) for complex $E$. If $E,F$ are Banach spaces over the same scalar field $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$ and $J:E\to F$ is a bounded injective linear operator ([math:def-bounded-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-linear-operator)), with operator norm as in [math:def-operator-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-operator-norm), then $J_*u(t)=Ju(t)$ defines a bounded injective map $L^p(E)\to L^p(F)$ with $\|J_*u\|_{L^p(F)}\le\|J\|\|u\|_{L^p(E)}$. Finally $\|u\|_{L^1(E)}\le T^{1-1/p}\|u\|_{L^p(E)}$, with $T^0=1$ when $p=1$, by [math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals).

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** Countable Choice, $0<T<\infty$, Lebesgue measure on $(0,T)$, $1\le p<\infty$, Banach spaces $E,F$ over the same scalar field $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$, and a bounded injective $\mathbb K$-linear operator $J:E\to F$.

**Goal:** Justify the normed classes, completeness, induced inclusion, and finite-interval estimate.

[F1] Strong measurability is an almost-everywhere limit of measurable simple functions ([math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)); scalar $L^p$ uses null equivalence classes ([math:def-l-p-space-as-a-quotient-by-null-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-l-p-space-as-a-quotient-by-null-functions)).

[F2] The real Bochner $L^p$ space is complete under Countable Choice ([Bochner $L^p$ is complete for a Banach value space](https://alphabetamath.cc/physics/item/thm-pe12-bochner-lp-completeness), [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)). Realification preserves the norm and metric ([math:lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm)).

[F3] Scalar Holder applies to the measurable norm and the constant function $1$ ([math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals)).

[F4] Bounded linear operators act between normed spaces over the same scalar field ([math:def-bounded-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-linear-operator)). Their operator norm is a finite bound: $\|Jx\|_F\le\|J\|\|x\|_E$ for every $x\in E$, including when $E=\{0\}$ ([math:def-operator-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-operator-norm)).

[F5] Under Countable Choice, the open interval $(0,T)$ is Lebesgue measurable and has measure $T$, by the one-dimensional open-box case of [math:thm-lebesgue-measure-of-a-box-of-every-kind](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-of-a-box-of-every-kind).

### Proof

1.1 If $s_n\to u$ almost everywhere are the simple approximants in [F1], the operator-norm bound in [F4] gives $Js_n\to Ju$ almost everywhere. The composed approximants are measurable simple functions, and $\|Ju(t)\|_F\le\|J\|\|u(t)\|_E$. Integrating the $p$th powers proves the asserted norm bound and independence of the representative. If $Ju=0$ as a class, pointwise injectivity outside its null set gives $u=0$ as a class. [F1, F4, given]

1.2 The displayed norm and quotient form a complete real normed space by [F2]. For complex $E$, the same simple functions, measurable classes and norm define the Bochner space over the realification. Completeness of its unchanged metric proves completeness over the complex field as well. Scalar multiplication and addition act on representatives and preserve the equivalence relation and strong measurability; the scalar triangle inequality for the norm is part of the real completeness interface. [F1, F2, given]

2.1 By [F5], $\int_0^T1\,dt=T$. For $p>1$, Holder with conjugate exponent $p/(p-1)$ gives $\int_0^T\|u(t)\|_E\,dt\le(\int_0^T\|u(t)\|_E^p\,dt)^{1/p}(\int_0^T1\,dt)^{1-1/p}=T^{1-1/p}\|u\|_{L^p(E)}$. For $p=1$ the assertion is equality. This proves the final estimate and completes the claims. [F3, F5, given, algebra] ∎


## Distributional time derivatives with values in a Banach space

[Open item](item-def-author-ab209e1f475e-banach-valued-time-derivative) · level 0


### Definition

Assume Countable Choice. Fix $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$, a Banach space $Y$ over $\mathbb K$, and $I=(0,T)$ with $0<T<\infty$. Equip $I$ with the restricted Lebesgue sigma-algebra $\mathcal L(I)=\{E\cap I:E\in\mathcal L(\mathbb R)\}$ and restricted Lebesgue measure $\lambda|_I$, denoted $dt$ in integrals. All null sets and almost-everywhere classes below refer to this measure.

Let $u,g:I\to Y$ be strongly measurable with $\int_I(\|u(t)\|_Y+\|g(t)\|_Y)\,dt<\infty$. Their integrals are Bochner integrals. Write $\mathcal D_{\mathbb K}(I)=C_c^\infty(I;\mathbb K)$: for $\mathbb K=\mathbb C$ this is the complex test-function space $\mathcal D(I)$; for $\mathbb K=\mathbb R$ it is its subspace of real-valued functions, with the restricted test-function topology. We say that $g$ represents the **distributional time derivative** $\partial_tu$ if
$$\int_Iu(t)\varphi'(t)\,dt=-\int_Ig(t)\varphi(t)\,dt\qquad\text{in }Y\quad\text{for every }\varphi\in\mathcal D_{\mathbb K}(I).$$
The sign is the scalar distribution convention; the pairing is bilinear, without conjugation of the test function.

The representing $g$ is unique as an almost-everywhere class. Its derivative bound is the Bochner norm $\|g\|_{L^1(I;Y)}=\int_I\|g(t)\|_Y\,dt$, not the supremum norm of a scalar pairing. The two maps on tests defined by the displayed integrals are continuous for the test-function topology. If $Z$ is a Banach space over the same scalar field $\mathbb K$ and $A:Y\to Z$ is bounded and $\mathbb K$-linear, then these identities transport to $\partial_t(Au)=Ag$ in the same sense. This includes bounded scalar-valued linear functionals and does not assert norm differentiability of $u(t)$.

### Definition references

[math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[Time translations and mollification in Bochner $L^p$](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-pe12-bochner-lp-mollification)

[math:lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm)

[math:def-bochner-integrable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bochner-integrable-function)

[math:thm-bochner-integrability-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bochner-integrability-criterion)

[math:lem-bochner-integral-norm-inequality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-bochner-integral-norm-inequality)

[math:thm-bounded-linear-maps-commute-with-bochner-integration](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bounded-linear-maps-commute-with-bochner-integration)

[math:def-test-function-space-d-of-an-open-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-test-function-space-d-of-an-open-set)

[math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative)

[math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space)

[math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)

[math:def-lebesgue-measure-and-the-lebesgue-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-lebesgue-measure-and-the-lebesgue-sigma-algebra)

[math:def-bounded-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-linear-operator)

[math:thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure)

[math:def-fixed-support-test-function-frechet-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-fixed-support-test-function-frechet-space)

[math:def-test-function-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-test-function-topology)

[math:lem-linearity-of-the-bochner-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-linearity-of-the-bochner-integral)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] Banach spaces have their specified real or complex scalar field and complete norm metric ([math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space)). Countable Choice is assumed ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)). The Lebesgue sigma-algebra and set function are defined by [math:def-lebesgue-measure-and-the-lebesgue-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-lebesgue-measure-and-the-lebesgue-sigma-algebra); under Countable Choice, [math:thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure) makes this a complete measure. Its restriction to $I$ is again a measure, since intersections with $I$ preserve disjoint unions and the empty set.

[F2] Strong measurability means almost-everywhere norm approximation by measurable Banach-valued simple functions ([math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)). The integral convention is [math:def-bochner-integrable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bochner-integrable-function); finite integrability of the norm gives Bochner integrability and approximation independence by [math:thm-bochner-integrability-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bochner-integrability-criterion). The norm bound is [math:lem-bochner-integral-norm-inequality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-bochner-integral-norm-inequality).

[F3] The complex test space and its real restriction are supplied by [math:def-test-function-space-d-of-an-open-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-test-function-space-d-of-an-open-set). On each fixed compact support its topology uses the suprema of the derivatives ([math:def-fixed-support-test-function-frechet-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-fixed-support-test-function-frechet-space)); seminorms continuous on each such support generate the test-function topology ([math:def-test-function-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-test-function-topology)). The scalar derivative sign and bilinear pairing are [math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative).

[F4] A bounded linear operator between spaces over the same field satisfies $\|Ay\|_Z\le C\|y\|_Y$ for some finite $C$ ([math:def-bounded-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-linear-operator)). Between Banach spaces it commutes with Bochner integration and preserves Bochner integrability ([math:thm-bounded-linear-maps-commute-with-bochner-integration](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bounded-linear-maps-commute-with-bochner-integration)).

[F5] The Bochner integral is linear, in particular on differences of integrable functions ([math:lem-linearity-of-the-bochner-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-linearity-of-the-bochner-integral)).

[F6] For a real Banach space, finite $T$, and $p=1$, time mollification converges in $L^1$ on compact subintervals under Countable Choice ([Time translations and mollification in Bochner $L^p$](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-pe12-bochner-lp-mollification)). Its proof supplies real, nonnegative smooth kernels of integral one supported in $[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]$. For complex $Y$, realification leaves the underlying set, addition, norm and metric unchanged ([math:lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-norm-realification-is-a-real-norm)), hence preserves completeness and strong simple approximation.

### Justification

**Given:** The scalar field, Banach spaces, restricted Lebesgue measure, Countable Choice, and integrability hypotheses in the Definition, with tests valued in that scalar field.

1.1 For $v=u$ or $v=g$ and $a=\varphi$ or $a=\varphi'$, the scalar factor $a$ is bounded and measurable. Take measurable scalar simple approximants $a_n\to a$ bounded by $\|a\|_\infty+1$ and the strongly measurable simple approximants $v_n\to v$ off a null set. Then $a_nv_n$ are measurable $Y$-valued simple functions converging there to $av$, since all scalars lie in $\mathbb K$. Thus $av$ is strongly measurable, and $\|av\|_Y\le\|a\|_\infty\|v\|_Y$ is integrable. The Bochner criterion gives all the integrals in the definition. Changing $u$ or $g$ on a null set changes no integral, by the same criterion and the norm inequality. [given, F2, F3]

2.1 The norm inequality gives $\|\int_Iu\varphi\,dt\|_Y\le\|\varphi\|_\infty\int_I\|u\|_Y\,dt$ and $\|\int_Iu\varphi'\,dt\|_Y\le\|\varphi'\|_\infty\int_I\|u\|_Y\,dt$, with the analogous estimate for $g$. The seminorm $p_1(\varphi)=\max(\|\varphi\|_\infty,\|\varphi'\|_\infty)$ is continuous on every fixed-support space, hence continuous in the test-function topology by its defining family of seminorms. Its restriction to real tests is continuous as well. These bounds prove continuity of the integral maps and give a well-defined vector-valued version of the scalar derivative sign rule. [F2, F3, step 1.1]

2.2 For $Z$ Banach over $\mathbb K$ and $A:Y\to Z$ bounded and $\mathbb K$-linear, applying $A$ preserves Bochner integrability and commutes with each integral. In particular, $Au,Ag$ satisfy the required norm-integrability hypotheses; their strong measurability also follows by applying $A$ to simple approximants. Since $A(u\varphi')=(Au)\varphi'$ and $A(g\varphi)=(Ag)\varphi$, applying $A$ to the defining identity proves $\partial_t(Au)=Ag$ with the same $\mathbb K$-valued tests. [given, F4, step 1.1]

3.1 If $g_1,g_2$ represent the same derivative, then $h=g_1-g_2$ is strongly measurable and integrable, and linearity gives $\int_Ih\varphi\,dt=0$ for every admissible test. Extend $h$ by zero outside $I$ and use the real smooth approximate identity $\rho_\varepsilon$ from [F6]. For $t$ in a fixed compact subinterval $J\subset I$ and sufficiently small $\varepsilon$, the real function $s\mapsto\rho_\varepsilon(t-s)$ is an admissible test in both scalar-field cases, so $(\rho_\varepsilon*h)(t)=0$ on $J$. For real $Y$, [F6] gives convergence to $h$ in $L^1(J;Y)$. For complex $Y$, apply that result to its realification, which is Banach with the same norm and metric by [F1] and [F6]; the convolution integrals agree because their defining simple integrals use only real scalar coefficients and the same norm limit. Hence $\|h\|_{L^1(J;Y)}=0$ in either case. A countable compact-interval exhaustion of $I$ proves almost-everywhere uniqueness. Therefore the derivative norm is independent of the representative. Countable Choice is used by the cited mollification and Lebesgue-measure suppliers. [F1, F2, F5, F6, step 1.1] ∎



## The L2 pivot injects into the local negative Sobolev space

[Open item](item-lem-author-ab209e1f475e-local-negative-pivot-injection) · level 0


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)), in particular the Countable Choice used by the cited Sobolev completeness and test-function density theorems. Let $G\subset\mathbb R^n$ be open, $n\ge1$, and let $\mathbb K=\mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$. Set $V=H_0^2(G)=\overline{C_c^\infty(G)}^{H^2(G)}$ with the full Sobolev norm of [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm) and closure convention [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure). Let $Y$ be its continuous dual in the real case, or its continuous conjugate dual in the complex case: $F(\alpha\varphi)=\overline\alpha F(\varphi)$ and $\|F\|_Y=\sup_{\|\varphi\|_V\le1}|F(\varphi)|$. The ordinary linear dual is [math:def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space). Then $V,Y$ are Banach by [math:thm-sobolev-spaces-are-banach-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-sobolev-spaces-are-banach-spaces), [math:lem-closed-subspace-of-a-banach-space-is-banach](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-closed-subspace-of-a-banach-space-is-banach) and [math:thm-bounded-operator-space-is-banach](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bounded-operator-space-is-banach). The map $J:L^2(G)\to Y$, $(Jf)(\varphi)=\int_G f\overline\varphi$, is linear, bounded and injective, with $\|Jf\|_Y\le\|f\|_{L^2(G)}$. Here [math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals) provides the bound and [physics:lem-smooth-compactly-supported-functions-are-dense-in-ltwo-of-an-open-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/physics/item/lem-smooth-compactly-supported-functions-are-dense-in-ltwo-of-an-open-set) provides injectivity. No compactness of $L^2(G)\to Y$ is claimed.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The open set $G$, the full $H^2$ norm including its zero-order $L^2$ term, and the declared scalar field.

**Goal:** Prove Banachness of the negative value space and justify its pivot embedding, including injectivity.

[F1] $H^2(G)$ is Banach under the stated choice assumption ([math:thm-sobolev-spaces-are-banach-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-sobolev-spaces-are-banach-spaces), [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)). The definition [math:def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-wkp-zero-as-a-sobolev-closure) places every test function in $H^2(G)$ and defines $V$ as its metric closure there. The full norm satisfies $\|\varphi\|_2\le\|\varphi\|_V$ ([math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm)). Once linearity of $V$ has been established, [math:lem-closed-subspace-of-a-banach-space-is-banach](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-closed-subspace-of-a-banach-space-is-banach) applies to this closed subspace.

[F2] The bounded linear operator space into a Banach scalar field is Banach ([math:thm-bounded-operator-space-is-banach](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bounded-operator-space-is-banach)); its norm on functionals is the dual norm ([math:def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space)).

[F3] Holder at exponent $2$, applied to the real-valued absolute values of representatives, bounds the absolute pairing ([math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals)). Smooth compactly supported functions are dense in $L^2(G)$ under Countable Choice ([physics:lem-smooth-compactly-supported-functions-are-dense-in-ltwo-of-an-open-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/physics/item/lem-smooth-compactly-supported-functions-are-dense-in-ltwo-of-an-open-set)).

[F4] Under Countable Choice, on real or complex $L^2(G)$ the formula $\langle f,g\rangle=\int_G f\overline g$ is a well-defined pairing of almost-everywhere classes. It is linear in its first variable, additive in its second variable and conjugate-linear there in the complex case, with $\langle f,f\rangle=\|f\|_2^2$ ([math:lem-l-two-with-the-integral-pairing-is-a-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-l-two-with-the-integral-pairing-is-a-hilbert-space)). The assumed Axiom of Choice supplies this Countable Choice, as it does for the other cited interfaces.

### Proof

1.1 The test functions form a linear subspace of $H^2(G)$: $0$ is a test function, and $a\varphi+b\psi$ is smooth with support contained in the compact union $\operatorname{supp}\varphi\cup\operatorname{supp}\psi\subset G$ for scalars $a,b$ and test functions $\varphi,\psi$. Let $u,v\in V$, $a,b\in\mathbb K$ and $\varepsilon>0$. By the closure criterion in [F1], choose test functions $\varphi,\psi$ such that $\|u-\varphi\|_{H^2},\|v-\psi\|_{H^2}<\varepsilon/(1+|a|+|b|)$. The norm axioms give $\|au+bv-(a\varphi+b\psi)\|_{H^2}\le |a|\|u-\varphi\|_{H^2}+|b|\|v-\psi\|_{H^2}<\varepsilon$. Thus $au+bv\in V$. Also $0\in V$, so $V$ is a linear subspace; it is closed because it is the metric closure of the test functions. [F1, given, algebra]

1.2 By [F4], $(Jf)(\varphi)=\langle f,\varphi\rangle$ is independent of the representatives of $f$ and $\varphi$, is additive in $\varphi$, and has the asserted linear or conjugate-linear scalar dependence. By [F3] and [F1], $|(Jf)(\varphi)|\le\|f\|_2\|\varphi\|_2\le\|f\|_2\|\varphi\|_V$. Thus it belongs to $Y$. Its dependence on $f$ is linear by [F4], and taking the supremum over the unit ball of $V$ proves $\|Jf\|_Y\le\|f\|_2$. [F1, F2, F3, F4, given]

2.1 By [F1] and step 1.1, $V$ is a closed linear subspace of the Banach space $H^2(G)$, so the closed-subspace theorem proves $V$ Banach. In the real case [F2] proves $Y$ Banach. In the complex case the map $F\mapsto\overline F$, with $(\overline F)(\varphi)=\overline{F(\varphi)}$, is a distance-preserving bijection from the conjugate dual to the ordinary linear dual. A Cauchy sequence in $Y$ therefore has a limit there, since its conjugates converge in the ordinary dual and the inverse conjugation preserves distances. [F1, F2, step 1.1, given]

3.1 If $Jf=0$, choose $\varphi_m\in C_c^\infty(G)\subset V$ with $\|f-\varphi_m\|_2<1/m$ for $m\ge1$, using [F3] and Countable Choice from the assumed Axiom of Choice. Each $\langle f,\varphi_m\rangle=(Jf)(\varphi_m)$ vanishes. By additivity and the diagonal identity in [F4], $\|f\|_2^2=\langle f,f\rangle=\langle f,f-\varphi_m\rangle$. The bound in [F3] gives $|\langle f,f-\varphi_m\rangle|\le\|f\|_2\|f-\varphi_m\|_2\to0$. Hence $\|f\|_2^2=0$, so $f=0$ as an $L^2$ class and $J$ is injective. [F1, F3, F4, step 1.2, given] ∎



## One flat reflection with simultaneous Lq and W1q bounds

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-ccbb64dfef2c-simultaneous-flat-reflection) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $n\ge1$ be an integer, $1<q<\infty$, and $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$. Write $x=(y,t)\in\mathbb R^{n-1}\times\mathbb R$ and $H_+=\mathbb R^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)$. Define one map $R:L^q(H_+;\mathbb K)\to L^q(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb K)$ by
$$(Rf)(y,t)=f(y,|t|)\quad(t\ne0),\qquad (Rf)(y,0)=0.$$
It is independent of representatives, linear, restricts to $f$ almost everywhere on $H_+$, and satisfies
$$\|Rf\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^n)}=2^{1/q}\|f\|_{L^q(H_+)}.$$
The same $R$ maps $W^{1,q}(H_+;\mathbb K)$ into $W^{1,q}(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb K)$ and satisfies
$$\|Rf\|_{W^{1,q}(\mathbb R^n)}=2^{1/q}\|f\|_{W^{1,q}(H_+)},\qquad \|u\|_{W^{1,q}}^q=\|u\|_{L^q}^q+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{x_j}u\|_{L^q}^q.$$

The compact-cylinder assertion is an almost-everywhere vanishing assertion: for any compact $K\subset\mathbb R^{n-1}$ and $a>0$, set $C_+=K\times[0,a]$ and $C=K\times[-a,a]$. If $f=0$ almost everywhere on $H_+\setminus C_+$, then $Rf=0$ almost everywhere on $\mathbb R^n\setminus C$. For $n=1$, $\mathbb R^0$ is the one-point space and the same conventions apply.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice). Its countable instances supply the choice hypotheses of the Sobolev conventions and change-of-variables inputs below; the imported half-space theorem uses AC through its ACL, one-dimensional absolutely continuous and Fubini inputs. The reflection formula itself is explicit.

[F2] [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm) defines real and complex almost-everywhere Sobolev classes, with the component $\ell^q$-sum norm displayed in the Statement.

[F3] [math:thm-wkp-extension-from-a-half-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-wkp-extension-from-a-half-space) applies with $k=1$, $p=q$ and coefficient $a_1=1$. Its candidate derivatives and interface cancellation (Proof, steps 2.1 and 3.2) give the tangential even and normal odd weak derivatives of this reflection, without imposing zero boundary values on $f$.

[F4] [math:thm-linear-change-of-variables-for-lebesgue-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-linear-change-of-variables-for-lebesgue-measure) implies that the linear involution $S(y,t)=(y,-t)$ preserves Lebesgue measurability and null sets, since $|\det S|=1$. Its singular-map clause also makes $\{t=0\}$ null: this hyperplane is the image of the singular linear map $(y,t)\mapsto(y,0)$.

[F5] [math:thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions) applies to the $C^1$ diffeomorphism $S:H_-\to H_+$, where $H_-=\mathbb R^{n-1}\times(-\infty,0)$, with $|\det DS|=1$. For any nonnegative Lebesgue measurable $g$ on $H_+$ it gives $\int_{H_-}g(y,-t)\,dy\,dt=\int_{H_+}g(y,t)\,dy\,dt$.

### Proof

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

**Goal:** Prove all stated conclusions for the single reflection map $R$.

1.1 By [F4], composition with $S$ on $H_-$ preserves measurability and carries the null exceptional set for equality of two representatives to a null set. The prescribed zero values on $\{t=0\}$ introduce no ambiguity, since that hyperplane is null. Hence the formula defines a class independent of representatives and is linear; its restriction to $H_+$ is $f$. Apply [F5] with $g=|f|^q$ and split the integral over the two half-spaces, obtaining $\int_{\mathbb R^n}|Rf|^q=2\int_{H_+}|f|^q$. This proves both membership in $L^q$ and the exact norm identity, before any differentiability is assumed. [F1, F2, F4, F5, given]

2.1 Suppose $f=0$ almost everywhere outside $C_+$ in $H_+$, and let $N\subset H_+\setminus C_+$ be its null exceptional set. On the upper half-space outside $C$ the reflected function vanishes except on $N$, and on the lower half-space outside $C$ it vanishes except on $S(N)$. By [F4] the latter set is null as well, so the asserted almost-everywhere vanishing outside $C$ follows. This is invariant under changing representatives. If $K$ is contained in an open chart base $B$ and $a<b$, then $C$ is a compact subset of the larger doubled cylinder $B\times(-b,b)$, so lateral and top margins are retained. [F4, step 1.1, given]

3.1 For $f\in W^{1,q}(H_+;\mathbb K)$, [F3] identifies the same formula with the $k=1$ half-space extension. Its weak tangential derivatives are $(\partial_{y_j}f)(y,|t|)$ for $1\le j\le n-1$, and its weak normal derivative is $\operatorname{sgn}(t)(\partial_tf)(y,|t|)$ away from the null hyperplane. The supplier's interface cancellation proves these identities on all of $\mathbb R^n$; the two zeroth-order traces match, so no surface term remains. Apply the integral calculation of step 1.1 to each derivative class, whose reflected modulus is unchanged by the normal sign. Each derivative's $q$th power norm doubles. Summing these identities with the identity for $f$ using [F2] proves the exact $W^{1,q}$ norm identity for this same $R$. When $n=1$ the tangential list is empty. [F1, F2, F3, F5, step 1.1] ∎



## Well-defined strip norm and the action of a common endpoint operator

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-ccbb64dfef2c-strip-model-well-defined) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $(X_0,X_1)$ and $(Y_0,Y_1)$ be compatible complex Banach couples: the two members of each couple are continuously linearly injected into a common Hausdorff topological vector space, with possibly different ambient spaces for the two couples. Give $X_0+X_1$ the sum norm $\inf_{x=x_0+x_1}(\|x_0\|_{X_0}+\|x_1\|_{X_1})$, and give $Y_0+Y_1$ the analogous sum norm.

On the closed strip $0\le\operatorname{Re}z\le1$, let $\mathcal F(X_0,X_1)$ consist of bounded sum-continuous functions, norm-holomorphic inside, with continuous $X_j$ boundary values tending to zero as $|\operatorname{Im}z|\to\infty$, and give it the maximum of the two boundary suprema. For $0<s<1$, the space of values $f(s)$, with the infimum of this boundary norm, is a well-defined Banach space $[X_0,X_1]_s$. The same construction and conclusions apply to $(Y_0,Y_1)$.

Let $T:X_0+X_1\to Y_0+Y_1$ be one complex-linear map, with $T(X_j)\subseteq Y_j$ and finite bounds $M_j\ge0$ satisfying $\|Tx\|_{Y_j}\le M_j\|x\|_{X_j}$ for $x\in X_j$, $j=0,1$. Then $f\mapsto T\circ f$ maps $\mathcal F(X_0,X_1)$ into $\mathcal F(Y_0,Y_1)$, and the same map $T$ restricts to a bounded operator satisfying
$$\|T\|_{[X_0,X_1]_s\to[Y_0,Y_1]_s}\le M_0^{1-s}M_1^s.$$

In particular, let $E:X_0+X_1\to Y_0+Y_1$ and $P:Y_0+Y_1\to X_0+X_1$ be single complex-linear maps with $E(X_j)\subseteq Y_j$, $P(Y_j)\subseteq X_j$ and finite bounds $a_j,b_j\ge0$ such that $\|Ex\|_{Y_j}\le a_j\|x\|_{X_j}$ and $\|Py\|_{X_j}\le b_j\|y\|_{Y_j}$ for every $x\in X_j$, $y\in Y_j$, $j=0,1$. If $PE=I_{X_0+X_1}$, their restrictions give bounded maps $E:[X_0,X_1]_s\to[Y_0,Y_1]_s$ and $P:[Y_0,Y_1]_s\to[X_0,X_1]_s$ with $PE=I_{[X_0,X_1]_s}$. Thus this endpoint retraction/coretraction pair remains such a pair after interpolation.

### External input

Reference 0, §2 before §2.1 (PDF pp.4–6), supplies the compatible-couple, admissible-operator and retraction conventions. Its §2.2 (PDF pp.9–10) states that the specified strip-function class is Banach in its maximum boundary norm, gives the vector-valued strip maximum inequality, and states the exact interpolation theorem for admissible maps between two compatible couples. These abstract facts are imported; the sum and evaluation quotient arguments and the applications to the specified common maps below are local compositions. No exterior extension result is included in this input.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses of the Statement and the independently cited external contracts.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice); in particular its countable and dependent instances are available.

[F2] [math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space) fixes complex Banach completeness; [math:def-bounded-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-linear-operator) fixes a common complex-linear map and its endpoint bounds.

[F3] [math:thm-quotient-of-banach-by-closed-subspace-is-banach](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-of-banach-by-closed-subspace-is-banach) applies to a closed kernel in a Banach space; AC supplies its Countable Choice hypothesis.

[F4] Reference 0, §2 before §2.1 (PDF pp.4–6), supplies the compatible-couple, admissible-operator and retraction conventions. Its §2.2 (PDF pp.9–10) states that the specified strip-function class is Banach in its maximum boundary norm, gives the vector-valued strip maximum inequality, and states the exact interpolation theorem for admissible maps between two compatible couples. These abstract facts are imported; the sum and evaluation quotient arguments and the applications to the specified common maps below are local compositions. No exterior extension result is included in this input.

[F5] [math:def-topological-vector-space-for-local-convexity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-vector-space-for-local-convexity) defines the ambient topological vector spaces, the continuity of addition and scalar multiplication, and the separate Hausdorff hypothesis used for each compatible couple.

### Proof

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

**Goal:** Prove the conclusions in the statement under its stated hypotheses.

1.1 The finite product $X_0\oplus_1X_1$ is Banach: a Cauchy sequence has Cauchy coordinates and coordinate limits, and the sum of their distances tends to zero. In the ambient topological vector space the map $(x_0,x_1)\mapsto x_0+x_1$ is continuous by continuity of the injections and addition. Hausdorffness makes $\{0\}$ closed, so its inverse image, the kernel of this map, is closed. The quotient theorem therefore makes the stated sum norm a genuine complete norm on the algebraic sum. The endpoint injections into the sum are contractive. Applying the same argument to $(Y_0,Y_1)$ gives its Banach sum and contractive endpoint injections. [F1, F2, F3, F5, given]

2.1 The imported strip theorem makes $\mathcal F(X_0,X_1)$ Banach. Its vector strip maximum inequality, applied in the sum norm, gives $\|f(s)\|_{X_0+X_1}\le\|f\|_{\mathcal F(X_0,X_1)}$ because endpoint injections into the sum are contractive. Thus evaluation has closed kernel. The quotient theorem applied to this kernel proves that its image with the infimum norm is Banach, independent of the choice of interpolating strip function, and the displayed evaluation bound makes its injection into the sum continuous and definite. This verifies the asserted definition rather than assuming its infimum is automatically a norm. The identical argument for $(Y_0,Y_1)$ establishes its interpolation space and continuous injection into its sum. [F1, F3, F4, step 1.1]

3.1 The specified common endpoint operator is bounded on the sums: for each decomposition $x=x_0+x_1$, $\|Tx\|_{Y_0+Y_1}\le M_0\|x_0\|_{X_0}+M_1\|x_1\|_{X_1}$, so infimizing gives a bound $\max(M_0,M_1)$ on the sum. It therefore maps a strip function to a bounded sum-continuous norm-holomorphic function, since bounded linear maps preserve norm limits and difference quotients. Endpoint continuity and decay are preserved by the endpoint bounds. For positive bounds $M_0,M_1$ apply this to $g(z)=(M_0/M_1)^{z-s}Tf(z)$. Both boundary norms are at most $M_0^{1-s}M_1^s\|f\|_{\mathcal F(X_0,X_1)}$, while $g(s)=Tf(s)$. Taking the infimum over $f$ proves the bound. If one bound is zero, use $M_j+\varepsilon$ and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. [F2, F4, step 2.1, algebra]

4.1 Apply step 3.1 to the maps $E:X_0+X_1\to Y_0+Y_1$ and $P:Y_0+Y_1\to X_0+X_1$ from the Statement, using their respective endpoint bounds $a_j$ and $b_j$ and swapping source and target couples for $P$. Their restrictions are bounded on the asserted interpolation spaces. For every $x\in[X_0,X_1]_s\subseteq X_0+X_1$, the assumed sum identity gives $P(Ex)=x$. Because these are the same maps restricted to the interpolation spaces, this is exactly $PE=I_{[X_0,X_1]_s}$. This proves the retraction assertion without replacing either endpoint map. [F2, step 2.1, step 3.1, algebra] ∎

### Source notes

The abstract strip Banach/maximal inequality is the explicit external input in reference 0. The quotient and common-operator applications are locally composed; no source proof reconstruction is claimed.


## One second-order flat reflection on L² and H²

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-891dd21c27d6-flat-second-order-reflection) · level 0


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $n\ge1$ be an integer and fix $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$; all function spaces below are over $\mathbb K$. Write $H_+=\mathbb R^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)$ with coordinates $(y,t)$. When $n=1$, $\mathbb R^0$ is a single point and $|y|=0$. Define one linear operator on $L^2(H_+)$ by
$$\mathcal Ef(y,t)=f(y,t)\quad(t>0),\qquad \mathcal Ef(y,t)=3f(y,-t)-2f(y,-2t)\quad(t<0).$$
It restricts to $f$ on $H_+$, is bounded $L^2(H_+)\to L^2(\mathbb R^n)$ and maps $W^{2,2}(H_+)$ boundedly into $W^{2,2}(\mathbb R^n)$. For $f\in W^{2,2}(H_+)$, the same formula matches the value and first normal derivative at $t=0$, in the weak trace sense used by the half-space extension theorem. For every $a,b>0$, if $f=0$ almost everywhere on $H_+\setminus\{(y,t):|y|<a,\ 0<t<b\}$, then $\mathcal Ef=0$ almost everywhere on $\mathbb R^n\setminus\{(y,t):|y|<a,\ |t|<b\}$. In particular these smaller bounds leave positive margins inside any larger doubled cylinder $\{(y,t):|y|<A,\ |t|<B\}$ with $A>a$ and $B>b$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice); in particular its countable and dependent instances are available. Its use here is inherited through the weak trace and integration-by-parts interfaces of the half-space supplier in F3; the reflection coefficients are explicit.

[F2] [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm) defines the weak-derivative $W^{2,2}$ space, its component-sum norm and almost-everywhere classes.

[F3] [math:thm-wkp-extension-from-a-half-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-wkp-extension-from-a-half-space) gives the $k=2,p=2$ reflection over either scalar field $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$ with coefficients solving $a_1+a_2=1$ and $-a_1-2a_2=1$. Its Facts F4–F5 and proof step 3.2 establish the required one-sided weak traces and cancellation of interface terms without assuming smooth-up-to-boundary density.

### Proof

**Goal:** Establish every assertion of the Statement under the stated hypotheses.

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

1.1 The moment equations have the solution $a_1=3,a_2=-2$, so the formula is precisely the $k=2$ operator from the half-space theorem. It already defines a map on every $L^2$ class: the two reflected coordinate maps preserve null sets. Changing variable $\tau=-jt$ gives $\|f(y,-jt)\|_{L^2(t<0)}=j^{-1/2}\|f\|_{L^2(H_+)}$, so the triangle inequality bounds the negative part by $(3+\sqrt2)\|f\|_2$. Adding the unchanged positive part gives a finite common $L^2$ bound. Linearity and the restriction identity follow from the formula. [F2, F3, given]

2.1 For a multi-index $\alpha=(\beta,m)$ with $|\alpha|\le2$, the candidate negative-side derivative is $g_\alpha=3(-1)^m(D^\alpha f)(y,-t)-2(-2)^m(D^\alpha f)(y,-2t)$. Its $L^2$ norm is at most $(3+2^{m+1/2})\|D^\alpha f\|_2$ by the same variable change. The positive-side derivative is $D^\alpha f$. The one-sided traces of order zero and one exist in the half-space theorem’s section sense, and their reflection coefficients are respectively $3-2=1$ and $-3+4=1$. In the half-space integration-by-parts identity the potential surface terms for second derivatives are multiples of these two mismatches; both vanish. Tangential derivatives remain on the boundary test function as in that theorem, so no derivative of an unconstructed boundary trace is used. Thus the fields $g_\alpha$ and their positive-side counterparts are the actual global weak derivatives. Summing their squared bounds proves the $W^{2,2}$ estimate for the very same $L^2$ map. [F2, F3, step 1.1, algebra]

3.1 Let $a,b>0$ and suppose $f$ vanishes almost everywhere outside $|y|<a,0<t<b$ in $H_+$. By the null-set preservation in step 1.1, both negative-side summands vanish almost everywhere whenever $|y|\ge a$ or $t\le-b$: in the latter case $-jt\ge b$ for $j=1,2$. The positive side is unchanged, and the hyperplane $t=0$ is a null set, so $\mathcal Ef$ vanishes almost everywhere outside $|y|<a,|t|<b$. If $A>a$ and $B>b$, these bounds retain margins $A-a$ at the lateral edge when $n>1$ and $B-b$ at the top and bottom of the larger doubled cylinder. For $n=1$ there is no lateral edge. This proves the localization assertion and completes the endpoint construction without an unproved density passage. [F3, step 1.1, step 2.1, algebra] ∎


### Source notes

The matching is justified by the exact existing weak half-space theorem, not by treating even reflection as an H² extension. No external extension theorem or fresh source-reading claim is used.


## Exterior and truncated obstacle domains

[Open item](item-def-se-geometry) · level 1


### Definition

Let $n\ge2$ and let $\Theta\subset\mathbb R^n$ be nonempty and compact, with smooth boundary. Compactness means that every open cover of $\Theta$ in its Euclidean subspace topology has a finite subcover. Set $\Omega=\mathbb R^n\setminus\Theta$ and require it to be connected: it has no separation into two nonempty disjoint relatively open sets.

Smooth boundary is understood in the one-sided smooth-obstacle sense: for each $x\in\partial\Theta$ there are an open neighborhood $W$ of $x$, a rigid motion $Q$, an open ball $B\subset\mathbb R^{n-1}$, and $h\in C^\infty(B;\mathbb R)$, with $Q(W)\subset B\times\mathbb R$, such that $Q(\partial\Theta\cap W)=Q(W)\cap\{(y,s):s=h(y)\}$ and $Q(\Omega\cap W)=Q(W)\cap\{(y,s):s<h(y)\}$. Equivalently, $Q(\Theta\cap W)=Q(W)\cap\{(y,s):s\ge h(y)\}$. The one-sided condition is part of the smooth-obstacle convention used here.

For $R_0>0$ with $\Theta\subset B_{R_0}(0)$, where $B_{R_0}(0)=\{x\in\mathbb R^n:|x|<R_0\}$, define the truncated exterior $\Omega_0=B_{R_0}(0)\setminus\Theta$, the outer boundary piece $\Gamma_1=\partial B_{R_0}(0)$, the obstacle boundary piece $\Gamma_2=\partial\Theta$, and $\Gamma=\partial\Omega_0=\Gamma_1\cup\Gamma_2$. All boundaries are taken in $\mathbb R^n$.

The standing hypothesis (H) additionally requires $\Theta$ to be non-trapping: every generalized reflected ray in the exterior leaves every compact set in finite time. This condition is imposed on the geometry; it is not needed to define the sets above and does not here assert a common escape time.

### Definition references

[math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)

[math:def-connected-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-connected-space)

[math:def-interior-closure-boundary-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-interior-closure-boundary-top)

[math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)

[Bounded $C^k$ domains and boundary charts](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-c-k-domain-and-boundary-charts)

[prop-se-generalized-ray-contract](item-prop-se-generalized-ray-contract)

### Justification

The one-sided graph convention is the local boundary-chart convention of [Bounded $C^k$ domains and boundary charts](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-c-k-domain-and-boundary-charts), used for every finite differentiability order. Only that local convention is used; no Sobolev interface or assertion that the unbounded exterior is bounded is imported. In the displayed chart the exterior occupies exactly one open side of the smooth boundary, as required by the domain-side hypothesis of [prop-se-generalized-ray-contract](item-prop-se-generalized-ray-contract). This is an explicit condition in the Definition, not a claimed deduction from a graph-only hypothesis. The generalized-ray and non-trapping convention, including glancing rays and without an assertion of a uniform escape time, is supplied by [prop-se-generalized-ray-contract](item-prop-se-generalized-ray-contract).

The sets in the definition are uniquely determined by set difference, the Euclidean ball, and topological boundary as in [math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball) and [math:def-interior-closure-boundary-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-interior-closure-boundary-top). An admissible $R_0$ exists: the traces on $\Theta$ of the balls $B_m(0)$, for positive integers $m$, cover $\Theta$; a finite subcover exists by compactness, and its largest radius contains $\Theta$.

Applied to the Euclidean metric, [math:thm-metric-hausdorff-separation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-hausdorff-separation) makes $\mathbb R^n$ Hausdorff. Thus [math:thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed) makes $\Theta$ closed, so $\Omega$ and $\Omega_0$ are open. The boundary identity follows locally. At a point of $\partial\Theta$, a sufficiently small ball lies inside $B_{R_0}(0)$, so $\Omega_0$ and $\mathbb R^n\setminus\Theta$ agree there; both $\Theta$ and its complement meet every neighborhood, giving a point of $\partial\Omega_0$. At a point of $\partial B_{R_0}(0)$, closedness of $\Theta$ gives a neighborhood disjoint from $\Theta$, and the ball and its complement both meet every neighborhood. Conversely, a point on neither boundary has a neighborhood lying entirely on one side of each of $B_{R_0}(0)$ and $\Theta$; its intersection with $\Omega_0$ is therefore either the whole neighborhood or empty. Thus no other boundary points occur. The two displayed pieces are disjoint because $\Theta$ is closed and contained in the open ball.

### Source notes

The original article supplies the geometry and notation at (1.4), and collects non-trapping and connectedness in (H). The explicit one-sided clause specifies the smooth-obstacle convention required by the independent boundary-chart and ray suppliers; it is a clarification of this generated definition, not a finding of a defect in the original article. Its citation is attribution for this article-origin definition; the justification above uses the stated mathematical suppliers.


## Zero extension of a free exterior tail by distribution tests

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-tail-distribution-extension) · level 1


### Statement

Assume AC and let $\Omega,\eta$ be as in [lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-bounded-collar-h2](item-lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-bounded-collar-h2). For $u\in H^1(\Omega)$ with $\Delta u=g\in L^2(\Omega)$, define $v=(1-\eta)u$ on $\Omega$ and $v=0$ on $\Theta$. Then $v\in H^1(\mathbb R^n)$, and its distributional Laplacian is the zero extension of
$$h=(1-\eta)g-2\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla u-(\Delta\eta)u.$$
In particular $\|v\|_{H^1(\mathbb R^n)}+\|\Delta v\|_2\le C_\eta(\|u\|_{H^1(\Omega)}+\|g\|_2)$. The support of $v$ need not be bounded.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice); in particular its countable and dependent instances are available.

[F2] [lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-bounded-collar-h2](item-lem-se-author-88d8498c76d6-bounded-collar-h2) fixes a smooth $\eta$ equal to $1$ on a neighborhood of $\Theta$; all terms multiplied by $1-\eta$ or derivatives of $\eta$ vanish on a smaller such neighborhood.

[F3] Weak derivatives and Sobolev classes use [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm), with compactly supported tests even though the function itself may have an unbounded tail.

[F4] [math:lem-weak-leibniz-rule-with-a-smooth-factor](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-weak-leibniz-rule-with-a-smooth-factor) supplies cutoff first derivatives, while [math:lem-test-function-cutoffs-and-euclidean-localization](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-test-function-cutoffs-and-euclidean-localization) supplies compact test cutoffs separating the obstacle from a given test support.

[F5] For an open set $U$ and $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$, [math:def-locally-integrable-function-as-a-regular-distribution](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-integrable-function-as-a-regular-distribution) supplies the regular distribution $T_f(\phi)=\int_U f\phi$, with a bilinear pairing and no conjugation. Using [math:def-distributional-derivative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-distributional-derivative), write $\Delta f=\sum_{j=1}^n\partial_j^2T_f$. Thus $\Delta f=q$ for $q\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$ means $\sum_{j=1}^n\partial_j^2T_f=T_q$, equivalently $\int_U f\Delta\phi=\int_U q\phi$ for every $\phi\in C_c^\infty(U)$. This convention applies on both $\Omega$ and $\mathbb R^n$. The $L^2$ classes used here have locally integrable representatives by the Sobolev conventions in [F3]; the assumed AC includes the Countable Choice required by the regular-distribution supplier.

### Proof

**Given:** The hypotheses and data in the Statement.

**Goal:** Prove the conclusions in the statement under its stated hypotheses.

1.1 The asserted fields $v$, the zero extensions of $(1-\eta)\partial_j u-(\partial_j\eta)u$, and the zero extension of $h$ have the claimed $L^2$ bounds by boundedness of the cutoff and its derivatives. All vanish near $\Theta$, so assigning zero on $\Theta$ gives well-defined almost-everywhere classes; no values of a representative on a boundary are used. [F2, F3, F4, given]

2.1 Fix $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$. The product $(1-\eta)\phi$ belongs to $C_c^\infty(\Omega)$, so the weak first-derivative identity on $\Omega$ gives $\int_{\mathbb R^n}v\,\partial_j\phi=-\int_\Omega((1-\eta)\partial_j u-(\partial_j\eta)u)\phi$. Thus the fields from the previous step really are the global weak derivatives, proving $v\in H^1(\mathbb R^n)$. [F2, F3, F4, step 1.1]

3.1 Testing $\Delta u=g$ against $(1-\eta)\phi$ and expanding its Laplacian gives $\int v\Delta\phi=\int_\Omega((1-\eta)g+(\Delta\eta)u)\phi+2\int_\Omega u\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\phi$. Integrate the last term once in distributions, with $u\nabla\eta$ supported away from $\Theta$, to obtain $\int v\Delta\phi=\int_\Omega h\phi$. This is the global Laplacian identity and proves its $L^2$ bound. Every test is compactly supported; no integration by parts at infinity or compact-support extension theorem is invoked. [F2, F3, F4, F5, step 1.1, algebra] ∎


## Admissible spectral strip profiles and the exact interpolation upper bound

[Open item](item-lem-author-03489e9088b7-spectral-analytic-strip-profiles) · level 1


### Statement

Assume AC and let $T\ge I$ be self-adjoint on a nonzero complex Hilbert space $K$. Give $D(T)$ the norm $\|Tv\|_K$. A compatible complex Banach couple $(X_0,X_1)$ means two complex Banach spaces with continuous injective complex-linear maps into a common Hausdorff complex topological vector space. Identify each endpoint with its image and form its algebraic sum there. For such a couple, use the sum norm $\|x\|_{X_0+X_1}=\inf_{x=x_0+x_1}(\|x_0\|_{X_0}+\|x_1\|_{X_1})$.

For a complex normed space $Y$ and an open set $U\subset\mathbb C$, a map $G:U\to Y$ is complex differentiable at $z\in U$ when there is a vector $G'(z)\in Y$ such that $\lim_{h\to0,\ h\ne0,\ z+h\in U}\|(G(z+h)-G(z))/h-G'(z)\|_Y=0$. It is norm-holomorphic on $U$ when this holds at every point of $U$. This definition also applies when $Y=X_0+X_1$ with its sum norm.

An admissible strip function is a bounded continuous function on $S=\{0\le\operatorname{Re}z\le1\}$ into $X_0+X_1$, norm-holomorphic in its interior, whose boundary maps $t\mapsto F(j+it)$ are continuous $X_j$-valued maps tending to zero as $|t|\to\infty$, $j=0,1$. Set $\|F\|_{\mathcal F}=\max_j\sup_t\|F(j+it)\|_{X_j}$; $[X_0,X_1]_\theta$ consists of the values $F(\theta)$ with $\|x\|_{[X_0,X_1]_\theta}=\inf_{F(\theta)=x}\|F\|_{\mathcal F}$, $0<\theta<1$. The sum quantity, the boundary quantity $\|F\|_{\mathcal F}$, and the interpolation infimum are norms on their respective vector spaces. In particular, every admissible $F$ satisfies $\|F(\theta)\|_{X_0+X_1}\le\|F\|_{\mathcal F}$, and hence $\|x\|_{X_0+X_1}\le\|x\|_{[X_0,X_1]_\theta}$.

For $(X_0,X_1)=(K,D(T))$, this convention gives $D(T^\theta)\subset [K,D(T)]_\theta$ and $\|f\|_{[K,D(T)]_\theta}\le\|T^\theta f\|_K$. More precisely, for $f\in D(T^\theta)$ and $\varepsilon>0$, the formula $F_\varepsilon(z)=e^{\varepsilon(z-\theta)^2}T^{-z}T^\theta f$ is admissible and $\|F_\varepsilon\|_{\mathcal F}\le e^{\varepsilon\max(\theta^2,(1-\theta)^2)}\|T^\theta f\|_K$. Here $T^z$ means the Borel multiplier $e^{z\log\lambda}$ on $[1,\infty)$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The spectral cutoff construction gives the domain norm, the spectral multipliers and the compatible domain couple [lem-author-03489e9088b7-spectral-cutoff-scale-identifications](item-lem-author-03489e9088b7-spectral-cutoff-scale-identifications).

[F2] The Borel calculus supplies bounded imaginary powers, exact norm integrals and convergence dominated in a scalar spectral measure [math:thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-unbounded-borel-functional-calculus).

[F3] AC is assumed for the spectral calculus, for selecting countably many approximate sum decompositions, and for the norming-functional theorem in [F5] [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice).

[F4] Banach means complete for the norm metric [math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space). A complex topological vector space has jointly continuous addition and scalar multiplication, and Hausdorff means distinct points have disjoint open neighborhoods [math:def-topological-vector-space-for-local-convexity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-vector-space-for-local-convexity). These are the underlying notions in the local definition of a compatible couple.

[F5] For a complex normed space $Y$, its dual $Y^*$ consists of bounded complex-linear functionals, with $\|\ell\|=\sup_{\|y\|_Y\le1}|\ell(y)|$ [math:def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dual-space-of-a-normed-space). Under AC, every nonzero $y\in Y$ has $\ell\in Y^*$ with $\|\ell\|=1$ and $\ell(y)=\|y\|_Y$; completeness of $Y$ is not required [math:thm-dual-norms-every-vector](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-dual-norms-every-vector).

[F6] For a bounded continuous scalar function on $S$ holomorphic in its interior, the supremum on an interior vertical line is at most the geometric interpolation of the two boundary suprema, including zero boundary suprema [math:thm-hadamard-three-lines](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-hadamard-three-lines).

[F7] Scalar complex differentiability is convergence of complex difference quotients, and scalar holomorphy means differentiability at every point of the open domain [math:def-complex-differentiability-holomorphic-and-entire](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-differentiability-holomorphic-and-entire). The norm-holomorphic definition above specializes to this definition for $Y=\mathbb C$.

### Proof

**Given:** The data and AC in the statement.

**Goal:** The norm assertions and the exact spectral-profile upper bound.

1.1 Write $\Sigma=X_0+X_1$ and let $V$ be the common Hausdorff topological vector space. The sum infimum is finite and nonnegative. Multiplying decompositions by a nonzero scalar and using the inverse scalar gives absolute homogeneity; adding decompositions within an arbitrary positive error of their infima gives the triangle inequality. Its value at zero is zero. If $\|x\|_\Sigma=0$, use AC to choose $x=a_n+b_n$ with $\|a_n\|_{X_0}+\|b_n\|_{X_1}<1/n$. The endpoint embeddings imply $a_n\to0$ and $b_n\to0$ in $V$. Continuous addition gives $x=a_n+b_n\to0$ in $V$. If $x\ne0$, disjoint neighborhoods of $x$ and $0$ contradict this convergence of the constant sequence. Hence $x=0$, proving separation. Thus $\Sigma$ is a complex normed space, and $\|x_j\|_\Sigma\le\|x_j\|_{X_j}$ for either endpoint by its one-term decomposition. [given, F3, F4]

1.2 Put $g=T^\theta f\in K$. Since $T\ge I$, $\|T^{-z}g\|\le\|g\|$ on $S$, and the sum norm of $(K,D(T))$ equals the $K$ norm: one inequality uses $\|x_1\|_K\le\|Tx_1\|_K$ and the other the decomposition $x=x+0$. The complex-power formula is therefore well-defined as a bounded sum-valued function after multiplication by the Gaussian. For $z\to z_0$ in $S$, the multipliers $\lambda^{-z}$ converge pointwise, bounded by one, giving norm continuity by the scalar spectral norm integral. For $\operatorname{Re}z_0>0$, the difference quotients converge in multiplier operator norm to $-(\log T)T^{-z_0}$ locally: on $\operatorname{Re}z\ge\eta>0$ the Taylor remainder is bounded by a constant times $|z-z_0|(\log\lambda)^2\lambda^{-\eta}$, whose supremum for $\lambda\ge1$ is finite. Thus the function is norm-holomorphic in the interior. [F1, F2, F3]

2.1 Let $F$ be admissible and put $M=\|F\|_{\mathcal F}$. For any $\ell\in\Sigma^*$ with $\|\ell\|\le1$, the scalar function $\phi=\ell\circ F$ is bounded and continuous on $S$. Indeed $|\ell(F(z))|\le\|F(z)\|_\Sigma$. At an interior point, applying $\ell$ to the norm-convergent difference quotients gives $\phi'(z)=\ell(F'(z))$, so $\phi$ is scalar holomorphic. At each boundary point, step 1.1 gives $|\phi(j+it)|\le\|F(j+it)\|_{X_j}\le M$. Three-lines therefore yields $|\ell(F(\theta+it))|\le M$ for every $0<\theta<1$ and $t\in\mathbb R$. If $F(\theta+it)\ne0$, choose its norming functional by [F5]; if it is zero the bound is immediate. This proves $\|F(\theta+it)\|_\Sigma\le M$. [F3, F5, F6, F7, step 1.1]

3.1 The admissible class is a complex vector space: sums and scalar multiples preserve boundedness, continuity, norm derivatives, endpoint continuity, and boundary decay. Its boundary quantity is homogeneous and subadditive. If that quantity vanishes, both boundary maps vanish, and step 2.1 makes every interior value zero, so it is a norm. Evaluation at $\theta$ is linear, so its range $[X_0,X_1]_\theta$ is a vector space. For nonzero scalars, multiplication is a bijection between the corresponding representing functions, giving homogeneity of the infimum. For $x,y$ in the range and $\delta>0$, choose representing functions with boundary norms less than their respective infima plus $\delta$; their sum represents $x+y$ and proves the triangle inequality after $\delta\downarrow0$. The zero function gives norm zero at zero. Infimizing step 2.1 gives $\|x\|_\Sigma\le\|x\|_{[X_0,X_1]_\theta}$; separation of $\Sigma$ then proves positive definiteness of the interpolation infimum. [given, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 On $z=it$, the multiplier $T^{-it}$ is unitary, by $|\lambda^{-it}|=1$. On $z=1+it$, the product/domain formula gives $TF_\varepsilon(1+it)=e^{\varepsilon(1+it-\theta)^2}T^{-it}g$. Both boundary maps are continuous in their respective endpoint norms by the same dominated spectral argument. Their norms equal $e^{\varepsilon((j-\theta)^2-t^2)}\|g\|$, so vanish at infinity and give the asserted upper bound. At $z=\theta$ the product identity gives $F_\varepsilon(\theta)=f$. Taking the infimum and then $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves the inclusion and exact upper inequality. [F1, F2, step 3.1, step 1.2] ∎


## Finite-time bounds for Banach-valued derivatives

[Open item](item-lem-author-ab209e1f475e-finite-time-derivative-conversion) · level 1


### Statement

Assume Countable Choice. Let $0<T<\infty$, let $Y$ be a real or complex Banach space, and suppose $g=\partial_tu$ in the Bochner distributional sense. Use Lebesgue measure on $(0,T)$ and the corresponding almost-everywhere conventions. Assume $g$ is strongly measurable and $\|g\|_{L^\infty(0,T;Y)}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t\in(0,T)}\|g(t)\|_Y\le M<\infty$. The least essential bound property and Hölder's inequality give $\|\partial_tu\|_{L^1(Y)}\le TM$. More generally, if $1\le q<\infty$ and $g\in L^q(0,T;Y)$, the Bochner norm convention gives $\|\partial_tu\|_{L^1(Y)}\le T^{1-1/q}\|g\|_{L^q(Y)}$. Consequently a uniform essential time bound, or a uniform $L^q$ bound, on a family's derivatives supplies a uniform $L^1(Y)$ bound with the displayed constant.

### Statement references

[lem-author-ab209e1f475e-bochner-lp-inclusion](item-lem-author-ab209e1f475e-bochner-lp-inclusion)

[def-author-ab209e1f475e-banach-valued-time-derivative](item-def-author-ab209e1f475e-banach-valued-time-derivative)

[math:def-essential-supremum-with-respect-to-a-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-essential-supremum-with-respect-to-a-measure)

[math:prop-essential-supremum-is-attained-as-the-least-essential-bound](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-essential-supremum-is-attained-as-the-least-essential-bound)

[math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals)

[math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space)

[math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)

[math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** Countable Choice, a finite interval with Lebesgue measure, a real or complex Banach value space, strongly measurable derivative representatives, and the stated uniform norm bound.

**Goal:** Convert the derivative hypothesis to the exact Bochner $L^1$ bound without changing its value space.

[F1] The derivative is the Bochner distributional identity, whose norm is the norm of its representative ([def-author-ab209e1f475e-banach-valued-time-derivative](item-def-author-ab209e1f475e-banach-valued-time-derivative)).

[F2] The essential supremum is an almost-everywhere bound ([math:prop-essential-supremum-is-attained-as-the-least-essential-bound](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-essential-supremum-is-attained-as-the-least-essential-bound), [math:def-essential-supremum-with-respect-to-a-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-essential-supremum-with-respect-to-a-measure)).

[F3] The Bochner norm convention and finite-exponent conversion are supplied by [lem-author-ab209e1f475e-bochner-lp-inclusion](item-lem-author-ab209e1f475e-bochner-lp-inclusion); the conversion is its last assertion, obtained by [math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals).

[F4] A Banach space has its specified real or complex scalar field and complete norm metric ([math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space)). Strong measurability is almost-everywhere norm approximation by measurable simple functions ([math:def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-measurable-banach-valued-function)).

[F5] Under Countable Choice, the one-dimensional open-box case of [math:thm-lebesgue-measure-of-a-box-of-every-kind](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-of-a-box-of-every-kind), with endpoints $0$ and $T$, gives $\lambda_1((0,T))=T$.

[A1] Countable Choice ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)) is assumed to meet the hypotheses of the derivative convention in [F1], the Bochner-space supplier in [F3], and the interval-measure supplier in [F5]. No necessity claim for the scalar norm estimates is asserted.

[F6] The endpoint $(\infty,1)$ case of [math:thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-holder-inequality-for-integrals) gives $\int_I|ab|\,dt\le\|a\|_{L^\infty(I)}\|b\|_{L^1(I)}$ for measurable real-valued $a\in L^\infty(I)$ and $b\in L^1(I)$, where $I=(0,T)$.

[F7] On $I=(0,T)$, the constant function $1=\chi_I$ has simple integral $\lambda_1(I)$ by [math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function). Its nonnegative Lebesgue integral is the same by [math:prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral). Thus [F5] gives $\|1\|_{L^1(I)}=T$.

### Proof

1.1 In the essential-bound case, put $h(t)=\|g(t)\|_Y$, the measurable scalar norm representative used in [F3]. The given bound and [F2] give $h\in L^\infty(I)$ with $\|h\|_{L^\infty(I)}\le M$. By [F5] and [F7], the constant function $1$ belongs to $L^1(I)$ with norm $T$. Apply [F6] to $a=h$ and $b=1$: $\int_0^T\|g(t)\|_Y\,dt=\int_I|h\cdot1|\,dt\le\|h\|_{L^\infty(I)}\|1\|_{L^1(I)}\le MT$. Strong measurability and this finite integral make $g$ the required $L^1(Y)$ representative in [F1], with the norm convention in [F3]. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7, A1, given]

2.1 For finite $q$, apply [F3] to $g$ on the same interval, under [A1]. This yields the displayed constant $T^{1-1/q}$. Applying either estimate separately to each family member and then taking the supremum preserves uniformity, proving the final assertion. [F1, F3, A1, step 1.1, given] ∎


## Uniform endpoint localization in a smooth boundary chart

[Open item](item-lem-se-author-ccbb64dfef2c-collar-chart-localization) · level 1


### Statement

Assume AC. Let $n\ge1$ be an integer, $1<q<\infty$, $\mathbb K\in\{\mathbb R,\mathbb C\}$, and let $\Omega\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be open. All function spaces below consist of Lebesgue almost-everywhere classes with values in $\mathbb K$; $W^{1,q}$ uses weak derivatives and the norm $\|v\|_{W^{1,q}(D)}^q=\|v\|_{L^q(D)}^q+\sum_{j=1}^n\|\partial_jv\|_{L^q(D)}^q$ on each indicated open set $D$.

Fix the following local graph data. Let $A(x)=Qx+c$ be a rigid motion, let $B_*\subseteq\mathbb R^{n-1}$ be an open ball, and let $h\in C^\infty(B_*;\mathbb R)$. Let $W\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be open with $A(W)\subseteq B_*\times\mathbb R$. For $A(x)=(y,s)$ define the ambient chart and its inverse by
$$\Phi:W\longrightarrow V:=\Phi(W),\qquad \Phi(x)=(y,s-h(y)),\qquad \Psi(y,t)=A^{-1}(y,h(y)+t).$$
Require the exact one-sided identity $\Phi(\Omega\cap W)=V\cap H_+$, where $H_+=\mathbb R^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)$. Choose open balls $B_0,B\subseteq\mathbb R^{n-1}$ with $\overline{B_0}\subset B$ and $\overline B\subset B_*$, and numbers $0<b<a$, so that $C=B\times(-a,a)$ satisfies $\overline C\subset V$. Put $C_+=B\times(0,a)$, $U=\Psi(C)$ and $K=\overline{B_0}\times[-b,b]$. Thus $K\subset C$ is compact and $\overline U\subset W$ is compact. These are local graph hypotheses only; $\Omega$ need not be bounded. For $n=1$, all bases are the one-point space $\mathbb R^0$, $h$ is constant and the same interval conventions apply.

Let $\rho,\psi\in C_c^\infty(U;\mathbb R)$, regarded as ambient smooth functions by zero extension, with $\operatorname{supp}\rho\subseteq\Psi(K)$. For $u\in L^q(\Omega;\mathbb K)$ define
$$(L_\rho u)(y,t)=\begin{cases}\rho(\Psi(y,t))u(\Psi(y,t)),&(y,t)\in C_+,\\0,&(y,t)\in H_+\setminus C_+,\end{cases}$$
and, writing $(Rf)(y,t)=f(y,|t|)$ for $t\ne0$ and $(Rf)(y,0)=0$, define
$$(T_{\rho,\psi}u)(x)=\begin{cases}\psi(x)(R L_\rho u)(\Phi(x)),&x\in U,\\0,&x\in\mathbb R^n\setminus U.\end{cases}$$
These formulas give well-defined linear maps $L_\rho:L^q(\Omega;\mathbb K)\to L^q(H_+;\mathbb K)$ and $T_{\rho,\psi}:L^q(\Omega;\mathbb K)\to L^q(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb K)$. Their restrictions map $W^{1,q}(\Omega;\mathbb K)$ boundedly into $W^{1,q}(H_+;\mathbb K)$ and $W^{1,q}(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb K)$, respectively. More precisely, for $X^0(D)=L^q(D;\mathbb K)$ and $X^1(D)=W^{1,q}(D;\mathbb K)$ there are constants $M_0,M_1$, depending only on the fixed chart, cutoffs and $n,q$, such that
$$\|L_\rho u\|_{X^j(H_+)}+\|T_{\rho,\psi}u\|_{X^j(\mathbb R^n)}\le M_j\|u\|_{X^j(\Omega)}\qquad(j=0,1).$$
If $\psi=1$ on $\operatorname{supp}\rho$, then $(T_{\rho,\psi}u)|_\Omega=\rho u$ almost everywhere. Such $\psi$ exists. The formulas use the same geometric data at both endpoints and may be fixed independently of $q$; the bounds may depend on $q$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The hypotheses and conventions of the Statement.

[F1] Assume the Axiom of Choice [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice). Its countable instances supply the choice assumptions of the Sobolev, weak-chain-rule, multiplication, zero-extension, change-of-variables, null-set and measurability inputs; reflection is used under its stated AC hypothesis. The local formulas are explicit.

[F2] [$C^k$ boundary flattening preserves local $W^{k,p}$](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-c-k-boundary-flattening-preserves-wkp-locally) supplies the $k=1$, $p=q$ weak chain rule and Sobolev composition bounds on matched compactly contained patches. Its Statement is for diffeomorphisms of arbitrary open sets; it does not require those sets to be bounded domains. The separate inputs [F8]–[F10] below supply the claims for arbitrary $L^q$ inputs.

[F3] [math:lem-test-function-cutoffs-and-euclidean-localization](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-test-function-cutoffs-and-euclidean-localization) supplies smooth cutoffs on compact subsets of open sets, including a $\psi$ equal to $1$ near $\operatorname{supp}\rho$. [math:lem-weak-leibniz-rule-with-a-smooth-factor](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-weak-leibniz-rule-with-a-smooth-factor) bounds multiplication by the fixed cutoffs on $W^{1,q}$; on $L^q$ their supremum norms suffice.

[F4] [lem-se-author-ccbb64dfef2c-simultaneous-flat-reflection](item-lem-se-author-ccbb64dfef2c-simultaneous-flat-reflection) gives the stated single map $R:L^q(H_+;\mathbb K)\to L^q(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb K)$, bounded on $W^{1,q}$ as well, with its compact-cylinder almost-everywhere vanishing assertion.

[F5] [math:lem-compact-support-zero-extension-in-wkp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-compact-support-zero-extension-in-wkp) gives zero extension for support compactly inside an open set, at orders $0$ and $1$. Before reflection, support can approach $t=0$; only the artificial edges are crossed, and the local test argument below establishes that partial extension directly.

[F6] [math:def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sobolev-space-wkp-and-its-norm) supplies the real and complex almost-everywhere class conventions, $W^{0,q}=L^q$, the weak test identity with bilinear pairing and no conjugation, and the displayed first-order norm, on arbitrary open subsets of $\mathbb R^n$.

[F7] The graph-chart convention is a local adaptation of [Bounded $C^k$ domains and boundary charts](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-c-k-domain-and-boundary-charts): only its one-sided graph geometry is used. Reversing the normal rigid coordinate converts its subgraph convention to the supergraph convention in the Statement. We impose the displayed local identity on $W$, not the supplier's global bounded-domain property on $\Omega$. The smooth graph shear and its inverse have absolute Jacobian determinant $1$ and bounded first derivatives on the fixed compact patches.

[F8] [math:thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions) gives $\int_{D_2}a=\int_{D_1}(a\circ S)|\det DS|$ for every nonnegative Lebesgue measurable $a$ on $D_2$, when $S:D_1\to D_2$ is a $C^1$ diffeomorphism between open subsets of $\mathbb R^n$, under Countable Choice. We use $a=|w|^q$, including for weak derivative classes, and the graph charts have absolute Jacobian determinant $1$.

[F9] [math:lem-c-one-diffeomorphisms-map-lebesgue-null-sets-to-null-sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-c-one-diffeomorphisms-map-lebesgue-null-sets-to-null-sets) says, under Countable Choice, that a $C^1$ diffeomorphism between open subsets of $\mathbb R^n$ maps Lebesgue null sets to null sets. Applying it to the inverse diffeomorphism also gives null preimages, which ensure independence from almost-everywhere representatives.
